starting point, scaled to whatever absolute size the scene calls for):

  Fox:    body 1.00, head 0.55, tail 0.80, ears 0.35, legs 0.55, eyes 0.08
  Wolf:   body 1.00, head 0.60, tail 0.60, ears 0.28
  Cat:    body 1.00, head 0.65, tail 0.90, eyes 0.12
  Tree:   trunk 1.00, canopy width 0.90
  Festival stall: base width 1.00, pole height 1.20, roof width 1.20

  Mech/Robot: torso height 1.00, torso width 0.70, head 0.30, upper arm
    0.65, forearm 0.55, hand 0.20, upper leg 0.75, lower leg 0.65, foot
    0.30, shoulder width 1.10

  Humanoid/Character: torso height 1.00, head 0.20 (deliberately bigger
    than the realistic ~0.125 ratio — a slightly oversized head reads
    more clearly and stays more expressive at low poly counts), neck
    0.06, shoulder width 0.85, hip width 0.55, upper arm 0.40, forearm
    0.35, hand 0.13 (also slightly oversized for readability), upper leg
    0.55, lower leg 0.50, foot 0.18

SEMANTIC TRAITS (what makes each subject recognizable):

  Fox: large ears, pointed snout, thin legs, long fluffy tail, orange and
    white coloring, slender body.
  Wolf: medium ears, long snout, muscular body, gray coloring.
  Cat: rounded head, large eyes, medium tail, small paws.
  Tree: vertical trunk, branch hierarchy, rounded canopy.
  Festival stall: rectangular base, four support poles, sloped roof,
    hanging lanterns, wood materials.
  Bridge: long horizontal deck, support pillars, symmetrical structure.
  Lantern: rounded body, hanging handle, warm glow.

  Mech/Robot: boxy/angular torso and limbs, sharply faceted plating (low
    detail, high contrast between flat panel faces), narrow waist
    relative to shoulders, oversized shoulder pads, glowing optic/visor
    (emissive), glowing joints or energy weapon (emissive, often a
    contrasting accent color against a neutral plating body), symmetrical
    limbs via bilateralSymmetry/mirrorX rather than hand-authoring both
    sides, panel-line detail via shallow engrave/boolean-cut grooves
    rather than dense geometry, weapons/shields built from a lathe
    (cylindrical barrel/hilt) or extrude (flat blade/panel profile)
    rather than a generic box.

  Humanoid/Character: heroic/stylized proportions (oversized head and
    hands per above, not realistic anatomy), broad shoulders tapering to
    a narrower waist/hips, limbs built from tapered capsules/cylinders
    with bendAngle on arms and legs rather than perfectly straight
    segments. Costume color-blocking (a chest emblem, a two-tone bodysuit,
    distinct glove/boot colors) is a HARD boundary, not a gradient —
    build each color region as its own primitive with its own baseColor
    rather than blending with vertex paint; reserve vertex paint (PART
    13) for soft, boundary-less detail on top of a region instead (grime,
    blush, scuffing, a worn patch). Eye lenses/visors/glowing emblems use
    emissive+emissiveIntensity, not just a bright baseColor. Armored
    joints suit a lathe; organic/cloth-covered joints suit a plain
    capsule/sphere. Capes/scarves/loose hair/flowing cloth use bend so
    they read as fabric rather than a rigid slab. Symmetrical limbs use
    bilateralSymmetry/mirrorX — author one arm/leg and let the mirror
    generate its pair.

CURVES AND SCULPTING:
  Avoid perfectly straight/boxy shapes unless intentional — curves create
  personality (a fox's tail bends, a tree branch is irregular, a lantern
  is rounded). Use large forms first, then secondary forms, then fine
  detail. Keep polygon count proportional to what each part needs (the
  Master Detail Directive above). Prefer strong, simple shapes over
  fussy ones for the primary silhouette.

PART 21 — VALIDATION CHECKLIST (run through silently before output)
================================================================================
  [ ] Using FLAT format: top-level "objects" array; every entry has "id";
      non-root parts have "attachTo"/"parent" OR "position" (not both
      with conflicting intent — PART 4); "material" (if present) is a
      STRING, never an object.
      — OR — Using NESTED format: root has "type":"group" and "children";
      every node has "name" (not "id"); "material" may be an object here.
      Never both formats at the top level.
  [ ] Every object/child has a valid primitive "type" from PART 2 (or is
      intentionally a "group").
  [ ] [v6.4] Every "type":"generated" object's "generator" is exactly
      one of: sphere, tube, lathe/revolve, extrude, disc/patch/oval,
      box_subdivided, cylinder, cone, capsule, spoked_wheel, smoke_trail
      — anything else silently becomes a sphere (PART 25). Each has real
      "params" sized appropriately, not just a bare "scale" left to the
      fallback.
  [ ] Every VISIBLE primitive has at least a "baseColor" (top-level field
      in flat format, or inside "material" in nested format). Exception:
      CSG tool objects — referenced in another object's booleanX "with"
      field and carrying no booleanX operation of their own — never
      appear in the final model and don't need a material.
  [ ] No baseColor/material/roughness etc. sitting directly on a "group"
      root or container node (groups have no surface of their own).
  [ ] Every extrude/lathe primitive has a "profile" array (PART 11).
  [ ] Every sdf_blob primitive has a "balls" array (PART 12).
  [ ] [v7.9] If the reference is one continuous sculpted/organic mass,
      the whole figure is ONE sdf_blob with multi-ball limbs (PART 61) —
      never separate primitives merely positioned to touch.
  [ ] Every boolean operation's "with" target id actually exists
      elsewhere in the model (PART 10).
  [ ] Phyllotaxis "children" templates use "name" (not "id"), and their
      "material" (if any) is a plain string, not an object (PART 17).
  [ ] No object has both "position" AND "attachTo"/"placement" set 
      the expectation that placement still applies (PART 4) — pick one.
  [ ] Hero/focal parts have raised "detail"; small/background parts
      deliberately don't (keeps their polygon cost low).
  [ ] Every cutout/vent/window/socket/notch/bolted-on lump that a single
      primitive can't represent cleanly uses a CSG boolean rather than an
      approximated overlapping shape.
  [ ] The model is immediately recognizable from its silhouette alone.
  [ ] All constraints/operations/materials/primitives used are from the
      lists in this document (PARTs 2, 3, 8, 9).
  [ ] [v6.4] Repeated geometry visible in the reference image (spokes,
      bolts, fence posts, window rows, track ties, gear teeth) uses
      radialRepeat or linearArray constraint instead of manually cloning
      identical objects — this is especially important when the reference
      shows symmetrical repetition clearly.
  [ ] All tube "path" arrays start near [0,0,0] — LOCAL OFFSETS, not world
      coordinates. path[0] ≈ [0,0,0]; the object's "position" puts the tube
      in the world. (See PART 26 tube ⚠ WARNING for wrong/correct examples.)
  [ ] smoke_trail "position" is set to the emission source in world space;
      startPos:[0,0,0] and endPos are LOCAL offsets (arc direction only).
      Smoke color set via "baseColor" or "color" param, NOT vertex baking.
  [ ] autoGround left at default (true) for ground-contact subjects — only
      set autoGround:false for intentionally floating/airborne models.
  [ ] Complex models (3+ repeated colors) define a "palette" block and use
      $varName references in "baseColor"/"material"/"emissive" fields.
  [ ] Blueprint sections annotated with // comments for readability.
  [ ] (PART 28) AI quality rules satisfied: no #000000 on visible surfaces;
      model fits in 3×3×3 units; generators used for non-trivial shapes;
      material presets match physical surface type from the reference image.
  [ ] v6.3 (PART 26): for rigged/posed characters, a "skeleton" + "joint"
      fields are used instead of hand-computed positions; "proportions"
      is decided before building parts, not after; organic parts use
      "smoothing":"auto" and mechanical parts use "flat"; "colorVariation"
      is OFF (omitted) by default — only set it when the reference image
      genuinely shows a hand-painted/textured surface, not reflexively;
      adjoining parts either set "overlap" or are sized
      0.04–0.08 units oversized to avoid seams; "contactShadow" is set at
      the blueprint level; panel-shade variants use "toneVariant" instead
      of new hex codes; a humanoid/animal pose has subtle "asymmetry"
      rather than a stiff mirrored T-pose; every flat surface over
      0.4×0.4 units has at least one secondary detail (panel line, bolt,
      trim, or vent).
  [ ] PART 27 (image fidelity): proportions/scale were MEASURED from the
      reference, not pulled from PART 20's generic tables, wherever the
      image showed them clearly; colors were sampled from actual midtones
      in the image, not invented; any posed asymmetry matches what the
      image actually shows; every part not visible in the reference is
      still built (occlusion inferred per 27.6), not left hollow/missing;
      no watermark, UI element, shadow, or compression artifact from the
      source image was reproduced as geometry or color.
  [ ] [v-next] Every id referenced by attachTo/parent/joint/sideOf/lookAt/
      operations[].with/"ref:" strings has been checked character-for-
      character against the ids that actually exist in this blueprint
      (PART 36) — including ids consumed by an earlier CSG "with".
  [ ] No object attaches to a rotated parent using plain "placement" —
      sideOf/sideToward used instead (PART 37).
  [ ] No object attaches to a CSG base/tool id for its final resting
      position (PART 38).
  [ ] This blueprint has been pasted into the renderer and the validation

  [ ] No "scale" value is 0 or near-0.
  [ ] Every limb (arm/leg) is ONE "chain"/"tube" mesh, or built via
      PART 45's derived-from-previous-segment math — never two+ capsules

  [ ] Every hand/foot/cuff/held-prop uses attachTo+placement targeting its
      limb's own id — none use a raw "position" or a stale skeleton "joint"

  [ ] Default standing pose has arms hanging roughly vertical with a mild

  [ ] Every object with no attachTo/placement/joint and no clear standalone

  [ ] Garment overlays (apron/vest/harness) have strap/tie geometry and

================================================================================
PART 24 — MESH PRIMITIVE (raw vertex/face geometry — added v6.1)
================================================================================
"type": "mesh" lets you supply arbitrary hand-crafted or algorithmically
generated geometry directly as vertex positions and face index lists.
This is the foundation of low-poly animals and organic models (shark,
dog, duck, etc.) where the shape cannot be expressed as any single
built-in primitive or combination of sculpted primitives.

REQUIRED FIELDS:
  "vertices"  array of [x, y, z] triplets — the mesh's vertex positions
               in LOCAL space, i.e. as if scale were [1,1,1]. The "scale"
               field on the object scales the whole mesh AFTER geometry
               is built (see COORDINATE TIPS below).
  "faces"     array of index lists — each entry references vertex indices
               into the "vertices" array. Supported polygon types:
                 triangle  [a, b, c]         — used as-is
                 quad      [a, b, c, d]      — auto-split into 2 triangles
                 n-gon     [a, b, c, d, ...]  — fan-triangulated from vertex 0

OPTIONAL FIELDS (all inherited from the normal object spec):
  "scale"          [x,y,z] — multiplied onto the geometry after build.
                  Use this to resize without touching vertex numbers.
  "position"       world position (PART 4)
  "rotation"       degrees (PART 4)
  "material"       string preset (PART 3)
  "baseColor"      hex color (PART 5)
  "roughness"      0-1 (PART 5)
  "metalness"      0-1 (PART 5)
  "opacity"        0-1 (PART 5)
  "emissive" / "emissiveIntensity"   (PART 5)
  "operations"     deform/array/CSG ops all work on mesh geometry (PART 9)
  "constraints"    symmetrical, radialSymmetry, etc. (PART 8)
  "attachTo" / "placement" / "anchor" / "offset"  (PART 7)
  "flatShading"    implicitly true — vertex normals are computed per-face,
                  giving the classic low-poly faceted look automatically.

WINDING ORDER:
  Faces must be wound counter-clockwise when viewed from OUTSIDE the mesh
  (Three.js default). If a face appears dark/invisible from outside, flip
  its winding: [a,b,c] -> [a,c,b].

COORDINATE TIPS:
  - Author vertices in a -1..1 local space and use "scale" to resize —
    this keeps vertex numbers readable and lets the AI describe shapes in
    unit-normalized terms.
  - Y axis is UP. Vertices at Y=0 sit on the ground plane.
  - Center the mesh at [0,0,0] in local space so "position" and
    "attachTo" behave predictably.

NORMALS:
  Computed automatically via computeVertexNormals() after geometry build.
  With flatShading:true on the material (which the renderer sets by
  default for low-poly style), each polygon face shows a single uniform
  color — the classic low-poly look. If you WANT smooth shading on a
  mesh primitive, add "roughness":0, "metalness":0 and the normals will
  blend between faces.

EXAMPLE — low poly duck body (simplified):
{
  "id": "duck_body",
  "type": "mesh",
  "scale": [1, 1, 1],
  "material": "cloth",
  "baseColor": "#8B5E3C",
  "position": [0, 0.5, 0],
  "vertices": [
    [0, -0.5, 0],
    [0.45, -0.1, 0.3], [-0.45, -0.1, 0.3],
    [0.45, -0.1, -0.3], [-0.45, -0.1, -0.3],
    [0.3, 0.4, 0.2], [-0.3, 0.4, 0.2],
    [0.3, 0.4, -0.2], [-0.3, 0.4, -0.2],
    [0, 0.6, 0]
  ],
  "faces": [
    [0,2,1], [0,1,3], [0,3,4], [0,4,2],
    [1,2,6,5], [1,5,7,3], [2,4,8,6], [3,7,9,4],
    [5,6,9], [5,9,7], [6,8,9], [7,9,8],
    [3,9,4]
  ]
}

EXAMPLE — low poly shark fin (a simple wedge):
{
  "id": "dorsal_fin",
  "type": "mesh",
  "scale": [0.4, 0.7, 0.15],
  "material": "flat",
  "baseColor": "#5a7a8a",
  "vertices": [
    [-0.5, 0, -0.5], [0.5, 0, -0.5],
    [0.5, 0,  0.5], [-0.5, 0,  0.5],
    [0,   1,  0]
  ],
  "faces": [
    [0,1,2,3],
    [0,4,1], [1,4,2], [2,4,3], [3,4,0]
  ]
}

COMBINING MESH WITH OTHER TECHNIQUES:
  A model like a low poly shark is typically built as a mix of mesh
  objects (the body, major fins) plus standard primitives (eyes as
  spheres, teeth as cones) plus bilateralSymmetry for paired fins/pecs.
  The mesh primitive handles the shapes that no built-in type can
  approximate; primitives handle the standard sub-parts.

VALIDATION CHECKLIST ADDITIONS (for "type":"mesh"):
  [ ] "vertices" is present and has at least 3 entries
  [ ] "faces" is present and has at least 1 entry
  [ ] All face index values are within range 0..vertices.length-1
  [ ] Faces wound CCW when viewed from outside (flip [a,b,c]->[a,c,b] if dark)
  [ ] Vertices authored in local space centered near [0,0,0]; scale applied
      via the top-level "scale" field

================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 25 — GENERATED PRIMITIVE SYSTEM (added v6.2, expanded v6.3.1, v6.4)
================================================================================
"type": "generated" is the PREFERRED way to build any organic, rounded, or
curved object. The AI writes generator name + parameters only — the renderer
produces mathematically correct geometry with proper winding, normals, and
topology every time. Never write raw vertices or faces for shapes that a
generator can handle.

v6.6 — THE FULL, EXACT LIST OF VALID GENERATOR NAMES (verified against the
renderer's buildGenerated() dispatch — anything else falls back to "sphere"
with a console warning, silently ignoring "scale"):
  sphere · tube · lathe (alias: revolve) · extrude (aliases: extruded_polygon,
  extruded) · disc (aliases: patch, oval) · box_subdivided (alias: box) ·
  cylinder (alias: cyl) · cone · capsule ·
  spoked_wheel (alias: spoked_wheel) · smoke_trail (alias: smoke_trail) ·
  lsystem (aliases: l_system, grammar) — see PART 30.5 for the full field
  reference on this one, it's meaningfully different from the others
  (rule-based turtle graphics rather than a fixed param shape) ·
  trim_band (aliases: trimband, band) — see PART 31.2.

There is no "ellipsoid", "torus", "ring", "wedge", or any other generator
name beyond this list — use a base PRIMITIVE "type" for those instead (PART
2), or build the shape from one of the generators above plus operations
(PART 9-15).

OBJECT STRUCTURE:
{
  "id":        "unique_name",
  "type":      "generated",
  "generator": "sphere" | "tube" | "lathe" | "extrude" | "disc" |
               "box_subdivided" | "cylinder" | "cone" | "capsule" |
               "spoked_wheel" | "smoke_trail" | "lsystem" | "trim_band",
  "params":    { ...generator-specific params... },
  "position":  [x, y, z],
  "scale":     [x, y, z],
  "material":  "fur" | "flat" | "glossy" | "metal" | "cloth",
  "baseColor": "#RRGGBB"
}

All standard object fields (rotation, opacity, emissive, attachTo, constraints,
operations, etc.) work exactly the same as on any other object type.

v6.3.1 — "scale" IS NOW A SAFE FALLBACK, BUT "params" IS STILL BETTER: every
generator below now falls back to "scale" (halved/used directly per each
generator's own dimension convention — same as the matching base primitive
in PART 2) for whichever of its own size params you omit, instead of
silently defaulting to a fixed ~0.5-1 unit blob that ignored "scale"
entirely. This means a missing "params" object is no longer catastrophic —
but "params" still gives you precision (and, for cylinder/cone, an
independent radiusTop/radiusBottom for tapered shapes scale alone can't
express) that's worth using whenever the shape needs more than a uniform
box/round bound.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "sphere"
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: head, body, muzzle, cheeks, eyes, any rounded organic blob.

PARAMS:
  rings         int   6–10    latitude rings (more = smoother). Use 8+ for heads.
  segs          int   8–12    longitude segments per ring.
  rx            float         x-axis radius (falls back to scale.x*0.5 if omitted)
  ry            float         y-axis radius/height (falls back to scale.y*0.5 if omitted)
  rz            float         z-axis radius (falls back to scale.z*0.5 if omitted)
  radius        float         uniform radius shorthand (overridden by rx/ry/rz)
  bottom_squish float 0–1     flatten bottom pole (good for sitting bodies)
  cheek_bulge   float 0–0.4   widen mid-band (good for cartoon heads/cheeks)
  top_flatten   float 0–1     flatten top pole

EXAMPLE — cartoon head:
{
  "id": "head", "type": "generated", "generator": "sphere",
  "params": { "rings": 8, "segs": 10, "rx": 0.50, "ry": 0.46, "rz": 0.44, "cheek_bulge": 0.12 },
  "position": [0, 1.54, 0.06], "scale": [1,1,1],
  "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

EXAMPLE — sitting body (flattened bottom):
{
  "id": "body", "type": "generated", "generator": "sphere",
  "params": { "rings": 8, "segs": 10, "rx": 0.52, "ry": 0.60, "rz": 0.46, "bottom_squish": 0.5 },
  "position": [0, 0.62, 0], "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "tube"
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: tails, limbs, tentacles, snakes, horns, any curved tube shape.
Uses parallel transport frames — no twisted geometry.

PARAMS:
  path        [[x,y,z],...] 2–10 points tracing the centerline curve
  radii       [r, r, ...]   one radius per path point (taper by reducing last values)
  radius      float         uniform radius if radii not specified
  taper       float 0–1     auto-taper from full to (1-taper)*radius along path
  segs        int   6–12    cross-section polygon sides
  cap_start   bool          close the start end (default true)
  cap_end     bool          close the tip end (default true)

EXAMPLE — fox tail curling upward and left:
{
  "id": "tail", "type": "generated", "generator": "tube",
  "params": {
    "path":  [[0,0,0],[-0.15,0.1,0],[-0.32,0.28,0],[-0.44,0.50,0.05],[-0.40,0.72,0.08],[-0.22,0.82,0.06]],
    "radii": [0.22, 0.24, 0.25, 0.23, 0.19, 0.08],
    "segs":  8
  },
  "position": [-0.60, 0.32, -0.34],
  "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

EXAMPLE — front leg (straight tube):
{
  "id": "leg_fl", "type": "generated", "generator": "tube",
  "params": { "path": [[0,0.18,0],[0,-0.18,0]], "radius": 0.10, "segs": 7 },
  "position": [0.28, 0.36, 0.40], "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

⚠ PATH COORDINATES ARE LOCAL (relative to the object's own "position" field).
  Do NOT write world coordinates in the path array — the tube will detach from
  the model if you do.

  WRONG (world coords — tube floats away):
    "position": [0, 0.5, 0.3],
    "params": { "path": [[0,0.5,0.3],[0.2,0.7,0.4]] }  ← duplicates position!

  CORRECT (local offsets — path[0] near [0,0,0], position moves it to world):
    "position": [0, 0.5, 0.3],
    "params": { "path": [[-0.1,0,0],[0,0.1,0],[0.1,0.2,0]] }

  RULE: path[0] should always be near [0,0,0]. Think of the path as a shape
  drawn around the object's own origin; "position" plants that origin in world.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: bodies with rotational symmetry — torsos, vases, bottles, lamp bases,
mushroom caps, tornadoes, any shape you'd make on a pottery wheel.

PARAMS:
  profile     [[r,y],...] 4–14 points from bottom to top (r=radius, y=height)
                          r must be >= 0. Vary r to shape the silhouette.
  segs        int 10–20   revolution segments (more = rounder)
  phi_start   deg         start angle (default 0)
  phi_length  deg         sweep angle (default 360 = full revolution)

EXAMPLE — rounded animal torso:
{
  "id": "torso", "type": "generated", "generator": "lathe",
  "params": {
    "profile": [[0,-0.6],[0.28,-0.5],[0.44,-0.2],[0.50,0.0],[0.46,0.25],[0.32,0.50],[0.10,0.60]],
    "segs": 14
  },
  "position": [0, 0.70, 0], "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "extrude" (alias: "extruded_polygon")
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: ears, fins, wings, leaves, feathers, shields — any flat pointed shape
extruded to give it thickness.

PARAMS:
  points    [[x,y],...] 4–12 outline points of the 2D shape (in XY plane)
                        Drawn CCW. The shape is auto-centered and extruded along Z.
  depth     float       extrusion thickness (falls back to scale.z, default 0.12)
  bevel     bool        add a bevel edge (default false)
  bevel_size float      bevel radius (default 0.02)

EXAMPLE — fox ear (tall pointed triangle with wider base):
{
  "id": "ear_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "extrude",
  "params": {
    "points": [[-0.22,-0.5],[0.22,-0.5],[0.26,-0.1],[0.10,0.6],[-0.10,0.6],[-0.26,-0.1]],
    "depth": 0.14
  },
  "position": [0.26, 2.06, -0.04], "scale": [1,1,1],
  "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#E8520A"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "disc"
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: chest patches, belly patches, paw pads, spots, coins, buttons —
any flat oval/ellipse patch sitting on a surface.

PARAMS:
  rings   int   3–6     radial rings (more = smoother edge)
  segs    int   8–12    angular segments
  rx      float         x radius
  ry      float 0.02–0.08  thickness (keep small — this is the Y axis height)
  rz      float         z radius (= rx for a circle, different for oval)

EXAMPLE — chest cream patch:
{
  "id": "chest_patch", "type": "generated", "generator": "disc",
  "params": { "rings": 4, "segs": 9, "rx": 0.22, "ry": 0.03, "rz": 0.18 },
  "position": [0, 0.65, 0.46],
  "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#F5E6C8"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "box_subdivided"
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: robots, vehicles, buildings, crates, any box that needs subdivision
for sculpt operations (taper, bend, twist, wave) or smooth rounding.

PARAMS:
  w, h, d   float   width / height / depth
  nx,ny,nz  int 2–6 subdivisions per axis
  round     float 0–0.3  corner rounding radius (uses RoundedBoxGeometry if > 0)

EXAMPLE — robot torso:
{
  "id": "torso", "type": "generated", "generator": "box_subdivided",
  "params": { "w": 0.7, "h": 0.9, "d": 0.5, "nx": 3, "ny": 4, "nz": 3 },
  "position": [0, 0.8, 0], "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#8899bb"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "cylinder" (v6.3.1)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: boilers, barrels, limb segments, pipes, smokestacks — anything
the base "type":"cylinder" primitive (PART 2) would build, but here under
"generated" so it can combine with sculpt params (taperAmount, bendAngle,
twistAmount) or sit alongside other generators in the same part. Identical
sizing to the base primitive when "params" is omitted — switching between
"type":"cylinder" and "type":"generated","generator":"cylinder" with the
same "scale" and no params produces the same result either way.

PARAMS:
  radius        float   uniform radius for both ends (falls back to scale.x*0.4)
  radiusTop     float   independent top radius — set this + radiusBottom for
                        a tapered shape (a funnel, a flared boiler collar)
  radiusBottom  float   independent bottom radius
  height        float   falls back to scale.y if omitted
  segs          int 6–24  radial segments (more = rounder)

EXAMPLE — boiler (the correct way to build a long cylindrical body —
note: "rotation" still needs to point its axis the direction you want; the
shape's own local Y is its length axis before any rotation is applied):
{
  "id": "boiler", "type": "generated", "generator": "cylinder",
  "params": { "radius": 0.21, "height": 1.35 },
  "position": [-0.35, 0.92, 0], "rotation": [0, 0, 90],
  "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#1F1F1F"
}

EXAMPLE — tapered funnel/smokestack collar (radiusTop ≠ radiusBottom — this
is the one thing the base "type":"cylinder" primitive can't do on its own):
{
  "id": "stack_flare", "type": "generated", "generator": "cylinder",
  "params": { "radiusTop": 0.30, "radiusBottom": 0.16, "height": 0.22 },
  "position": [0, 1.95, 0], "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#1A1A1A"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "cone" (v6.3.1)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: spikes, horns, party hats, tree-tops, drill bits, rocket nose
cones — identical sizing to the base "type":"cone" primitive when params
are omitted.

PARAMS:
  radius   float   base radius (falls back to scale.x*0.5)
  height   float   falls back to scale.y if omitted
  segs     int 6–24  radial segments

EXAMPLE — party-hat spike:
{
  "id": "hat_spike", "type": "generated", "generator": "cone",
  "params": { "radius": 0.18, "height": 0.45 },
  "position": [0, 1.7, 0], "material": "flat", "baseColor": "#E94560"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "capsule" (v6.3.1)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: rounded limb segments, pill-shaped pods, fingers, sausage-link
chains — identical sizing to the base "type":"capsule" primitive when
params are omitted.

PARAMS:
  radius      float   falls back to min(scale.x,scale.z)*0.4
  length      float   straight-section length, falls back to a value
                       derived from scale.y minus the rounded end-caps
  segs        int 4–12   cap-rounding segments
  radialSegs  int 6–20   segments around the circumference

EXAMPLE — hand riding the end of an arm (attachTo + placement — NEVER a
hand-typed position/rotation for a limb-riding part; see PART 40/41):
{
  "id": "hand_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "capsule",
  "params": { "radius": 0.09, "length": 0.02 },
  "attachTo": "arm_l", "placement": "below",
  "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8c39e"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "spoked_wheel" (v6.4)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: train wheels, bicycle wheels, cart wheels, fans — a complete rim +
hub + spokes merged into one geometry. Default orientation is Y-up (same as
type:"cylinder"). Use rotation:[90,0,0] to point sideways.

IMAGE NOTE: When the reference shows any wheeled vehicle, use this generator
for every drive/idle wheel visible. Count the spokes from the image and set
spokeCount to match. Read rimRadius from the visual scale of the wheel
relative to the model height.

PARAMS:
  rimRadius      float   outer radius of the wheel (default 0.28)
  hubRadius      float   radius of the centre hub cylinder (default 0.06)
  rimThickness   float   radial depth of the rim ring (default 0.08)
  spokeCount     int     number of spokes (default 6)
  spokeWidth     float   spoke cross-section width (default 0.03)
  wheelDepth     float   axle-direction thickness of the whole wheel (default 0.10)

EXAMPLE — train drive wheel:
{
  "id": "drive_wheel_l",
  "type": "generated",
  "generator": "spoked_wheel",
  "params": { "rimRadius": 0.28, "spokeCount": 8,
              "hubRadius": 0.06, "rimThickness": 0.06 },
  "position": [0, 0.32, 0.36],
  "rotation": [90, 0, 0],
  "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#4A4A4A"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GENERATOR: "smoke_trail" (v6.4)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Best for: steam plumes, exhaust clouds, chimney smoke — replaces 6–10 manual
sphere objects with one clean declaration. Puff arc follows a quadratic
Bezier curve, bowing upward by arcHeight.

⚠ POSITION / COORD SYSTEM: startPos and endPos are LOCAL OFFSETS relative
  to the object's "position" field — NOT world coordinates. Place the object
  at the emission source (chimney tip, exhaust port), then use
  startPos:[0,0,0] as the start and endPos as the arc direction/distance.

  WRONG (world coords in startPos — smoke appears at double-height):
    "position": [0, 2.05, 0],
    "params": { "startPos": [0, 2.05, 0], "endPos": [1, 3.1, 0.15] }

  CORRECT (local offsets — start AT the object position, arc from there):
    "position": [0, 2.05, 0],
    "params": { "startPos": [0, 0, 0], "endPos": [1.0, 1.1, 0.15] }

IMAGE NOTE: Read the chimney/exhaust tip's world position for "position".
  Read the smoke color from the image ("color": "#E0E0E0" for white steam,
  "#c8bfb0" for light coal smoke, "#333333" for dark diesel exhaust).
  endPos direction should match the arc visible in the reference image.

COLOR: set "baseColor" on the object (or use the "color" param as an alias).
  Do NOT rely on vertex-colour baking — use baseColor/color directly.

OPACITY: "startOpacity" sets the whole trail's uniform opacity (default 0.88).

PARAMS:
  count        int     number of puff spheres (default 8)
  startPos     [x,y,z] LOCAL OFFSET of the first puff (use [0,0,0])
  endPos       [x,y,z] LOCAL OFFSET of the arc tip (direction + distance)
  startRadius  float   radius of the first/largest puff (default 0.22)
  endRadius    float   radius of the last/smallest puff (default 0.12)
  startOpacity float   uniform opacity of the whole trail (default 0.88)
  color        hex     alias for baseColor smoke colour (default "#E0E0E0")
  arcHeight    float   how much the arc bows upward vs. straight (default 0.2)

EXAMPLE — steam plume from a locomotive chimney:
{
  "id": "steam_smoke",
  "type": "generated",
  "generator": "smoke_trail",
  "position": [0, 2.05, 0],
  "params": {
    "count": 8,
    "startPos": [0, 0, 0],
    "endPos":   [1.0, 1.1, 0.15],
    "startRadius": 0.22, "endRadius": 0.10,
    "startOpacity": 0.85,
    "color": "#E0E0E0", "arcHeight": 0.30
  },
  "material": "default",
  "baseColor": "#E0E0E0"
}

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHICH GENERATOR TO USE — QUICK REFERENCE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  Part type                  → Generator
  ─────────────────────────────────────────
  Head, muzzle, body, cheeks → sphere  (cheek_bulge for cartoon heads)
  Sitting body (flat bottom) → sphere  (bottom_squish: 0.4–0.6)
  Tail, limbs, tentacles     → tube    (curved path + tapering radii)
  Torso, vase-like body      → lathe   (profile curve)
  Ears, fins, wings, leaves  → extrude (alias: extruded_polygon)
  Chest/belly color patch    → disc
  Robots, crates, buildings  → box_subdivided  (or just use "cube" primitive)
  Boiler, barrel, pipe, limb → cylinder  (or "type":"cylinder" primitive if no params needed)
  Tapered funnel/collar      → cylinder  (radiusTop ≠ radiusBottom)
  Spike, horn, party hat     → cone
  Rounded limb/finger/pod    → capsule
  Spoked wheel, fan, gear    → spoked_wheel  (v6.4 — read spokeCount from image)
  Steam/smoke/exhaust plume  → smoke_trail   (v6.4 — replaces 6–10 manual spheres)
  Eyes, nose, tiny details   → sphere or ellipsoid PRIMITIVE (no generator)

WHEN STILL USE "type":"mesh" (raw vertices):
  Only when no generator can produce the shape — e.g. a very specific
  hand-crafted silhouette. Always prefer "generated" first.

WHEN STILL USE PRIMITIVES (sphere, ellipsoid, cone, capsule, etc.):
  For tiny details: eyes, nose, shine dots, claws, teeth. These are
  simple enough that a primitive is fine and faster.

COORDINATE SYSTEM FOR GENERATED OBJECTS:
  - Y=0 is ground. Feet at Y≈0.05–0.15.
  - Full model fits in 2×2×2 unit bounding box.
  - Body center Y≈0.55–0.70, Head center Y≈1.45–1.65.
  - Positive Z faces toward the camera (front of model).
  - Paired parts (ear_l/ear_r) mirror at ±X.

AI GENERATION RULES:
  1. Analyze the reference image BEFORE writing JSON.
  2. Pick the right generator per part using the table above.
  3. For "tube" tails: path must actually curve — minimum 5 path points.
  4. For "sphere" heads: always use rings≥7, segs≥10.
  5. For "extrude" ears: points must form a sensible 2D outline.
  6. For "disc" patches: ry should be 0.02–0.06 (very flat).
  7. NEVER write "vertices" or "faces" arrays when using "generated".
  8. Every part needs a unique "id".
  9. Paired parts (l/r) must be at ±X mirror positions.
  10. Eyes: always "type":"sphere", "material":"glossy" — not "generated".
  11. [v6.4] Only use a generator name from this PART's exact list (sphere,
      tube, lathe/revolve, extrude, disc, box_subdivided, cylinder, cone,
      capsule, spoked_wheel, smoke_trail) — anything else silently becomes
      a sphere. Prefer giving each generator its own real "params" over
      relying on scale-fallback.
  12. [v6.4] Use radialRepeat/linearArray constraints for repeated geometry
      visible in the reference image rather than writing identical objects
      by hand (PART 8).

================================================================================
================================================================================
PART 26 — ADVANCED MODEL QUALITY SYSTEM (added v6.3)
================================================================================
Ten upgrades aimed at the gap between "geometrically correct" and "actually
looks hand-crafted." 1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 are renderer features (verified
against real code, listed below). 2 is a UI-only display. 9, 10 are pure
JSON/authoring conventions — no renderer change, just rules for you to
follow when generating a model. 8 (asymmetry) STARTED as a pure authoring
convention too, but PART 34-R.7 (v7.3) made 4 of its keys a live renderer
feature — see 26.8 below and PART 34-R.7 for the current, corrected story.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.1 — SKELETON SYSTEM (verified against buildScene()'s joint-resolution
       step, just before solvePosition())
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
An optional blueprint-level "skeleton" object names joints and their
world coordinates. Any object can ride a joint instead of specifying its
own "position":

  "skeleton": { "<jointName>": [x, y, z], ... }     — blueprint-level
  "joint": "<jointName>"                            — per-object

If the object has "joint" and that name exists in blueprint.skeleton, the
joint's coordinate is copied onto the object's "position" field — meaning
it then behaves exactly like an explicit "position" (PART 4's precedence
rule applies: this OVERRIDES attachTo/placement/anchor for that object,
same as writing the coordinate by hand). An object with no "joint" field
is completely unaffected — skeleton and non-skeleton objects can be mixed
freely in the same blueprint.

A "Show Skeleton" toggle in the toolbar reveals small grey marker spheres
(radius 0.04) at every joint, purely for visual debugging. These markers
are never part of sceneObjects, never exported (GLB or OBJ), and never
selectable in the Inspector/scene tree.

Use this for any rigged/posed character or robot — author the skeleton
once, then every part just says which joint it rides, instead of every
limb computing its own world position by hand.

[RJS update 1, S2] When meta.skeleton is present and the blueprint uses
the TS path, the renderer's T-Pose button can reset joint rotations to
a neutral rest pose.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.2 — PROPORTION LOCK (UI display only — no renderer geometry effect)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
An optional blueprint-level "proportions" object records the target
measurements you designed the model around:

  "proportions": {
    "total_height": 2.0, "head_h": 0.18, "body_h": 0.30,
    "shoulder_w": 0.60, "arm_length": 0.45, "leg_length": 0.28,
    "foot_y": 0.0
  }

This is informational only — the renderer displays it in the Inspector
panel for reference but never auto-corrects geometry against it. Its
purpose is AI self-consistency: decide these numbers FIRST, then build
every part to match them, instead of drifting part-by-part.

[RJS update 1, S1] "meta.intent" : "game-asset" | "product-viz" |
  "3d-print" | "character" | "environment" | string
  Optional. The renderer MAY read this to set sensible defaults
  (materials, export tier, validation strictness). If absent, no
  special behavior is applied.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.3 — SMOOTHING GROUPS (verified against applySmoothingGroups() /
       buildMaterial())
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "smoothing": "flat" | "smooth" | "auto" | <number 0–180>

  flat (default, omit the field entirely for this) — faceted low-poly
       look, exactly the old unconditional behavior. No change if unset.
  smooth — every coincident vertex blends into one smooth normal
       (threshold 180°, i.e. nothing stays hard).
  auto — smooths across faces meeting at under 30°, keeps a hard edge at
       30° or sharper. Good default for "smooth curves, hard corners."
  <number> — same idea as "auto" but with your own angle threshold in
       degrees (clamped to 0–180).

RULE: organic parts (fur, skin, flesh, fabric) → "smoothing":"auto".
      mechanical parts (armor, robots, vehicles) → "smoothing":"flat"
      (or just omit it).

Live-editable in the Inspector (dropdown) when an object is selected, so
you can compare flat vs. smooth vs. auto on the actual model without
re-pasting JSON.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.4 — FACE COLOR VARIATION (verified against applyColorVariation() /
       vertex-color path in buildMaterial())
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "colorVariation": 0.0–0.25   (default: 0 / omitted — OFF)

DEFAULT IS OFF (v6.6). Only add this field when the reference image
actually shows a hand-painted/textured/weathered surface — don't add it
out of habit. A flat, evenly-lit surface matching the image's real
shading is the correct default, not something to compensate for.

  0.04–0.08   → the image shows subtle hand-painted/weathered texture
  0.08–0.15   → the image shows heavy texture/wear (rust, fur, foliage)

Live-editable in the Inspector (slider, 0.00–0.25) when an object is
selected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.5 — PART OVERLAP / PADDING (verified against the overlap scale-up step
       in buildScene(), applied right after each mesh's transform is set)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "overlap": 0.0–0.15

Scales the object up uniformly by (1 + overlap) around its own position,
so its edges push slightly into whatever it's touching — hides the
hairline gaps that show up between adjoining low-poly parts (shoulder/
torso, head/neck, etc.) from some camera angles. Clamped to 0–0.15;
larger values visibly bloat the part instead of just sealing a seam.

This can ALSO be done purely as an authoring convention without the
field at all: size adjoining parts 0.04–0.08 units larger than the exact
geometric fit, so they already sink into their neighbor. Use whichever
is more convenient per-part; they produce the same visual result.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.6 — CONTACT SHADOW (verified against the contact-shadow disc built in
       finalizeSceneBuild())
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "contactShadow": true
  "contactShadow": { "opacity": 0.35, "blur": 1.2, "scale": 1.0 }

A soft dark ellipse rendered on the ground directly under the model,
auto-sized to the model's actual XZ footprint (with a small pad so it
doesn't look clipped at the edges). Fake but effective — adds visual
weight/grounding with zero geometry cost. `true` uses the defaults shown
above. All three sub-fields are optional and independently overridable:
  opacity  0–1     how dark the shadow is at its center
  blur     ~0.3–3  how soft/wide the edge falloff is (higher = softer)
  scale    float   uniform multiplier on the auto-computed footprint size

A "Contact Shadow" toggle in the toolbar lets the viewer turn it on/off
without touching the JSON. The disc is never part of sceneObjects and is
never exported (GLB or OBJ) — purely a viewport visual aid.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.7 — MATERIAL TONE VARIANT (verified against applyToneVariant() /
       buildMaterial())
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "toneVariant": "light" | "mid" | "dark" | "highlight"

Adjusts the resolved base color's lightness via HSL, so panels can be
described relative to a main color instead of needing their own exact
hex code:
  light      lightness +15%
  mid        unchanged (the default if the field is omitted)
  dark       lightness −15%
  highlight  lightness +25%, saturation +10% (for glowing/lit-up trim,
             separate from true "emissive" — combine both if you want a
             trim that's both lit-looking AND actually emissive)

Applies AFTER "color"/"baseColor" is resolved, so it's a pure adjustment
on top of whatever color you specified — you don't need to compute the
lighter/darker hex yourself.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.8 — ASYMMETRY (live for 4 suffixes as of v7.3 — see PART 34-R.7)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
IMAGE-TO-JS NOTE: when a reference image is available, use PART 27.3
instead of the guidance below — measure the actual asymmetry shown in the
image and reproduce it at that magnitude, rather than inventing a small
random offset. The guidance below is the prompt-only fallback.

  "asymmetry": {
    "head_tilt": 3, "shoulder_l_drop": 0.04
  }

A blueprint-level note-to-self for the AI: small intentional asymmetries
read as a natural pose instead of a stiff, perfectly mirrored T-pose.
[CORRECTED, v7.8/PART 54 — this section originally said "There is no code
that reads this object" and its example used an "arm_r_forward" suffix.
Both were wrong/stale by the time PART 34-R.7 (v7.3) shipped: the renderer
DOES read this object now, automatically, but only for exactly four
suffixes — "_drop"/"_lift"/"_shift"/"_tilt" (see PART 34-R.7 for the
mechanism). "_forward" was never one of them, so that part of the old
example silently did nothing even after v7.3 — it's been replaced above
with a live suffix.] For anything the four live suffixes can't express —
reshaping a mesh, not just moving/tilting the joint it rides on — keep
using this field as a documentation-only note-to-self and apply the actual
change directly on the object's own "rotation"/"position"/params, same as
before.

When generating a pose needing MORE than the four live suffixes above,
consider applying (directly on the relevant objects' "rotation"/"position"):
  arm pose variation   one arm 0.04–0.08 units more forward (Z offset)
  weight shift         hips rotated 2–4° on Z, toward one side
RULE: keep all of these SUBTLE — never more than 5–8° rotation or 0.08
units of position offset. The goal is "alive," not "broken rig."

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.9 — SILHOUETTE QUALITY RULES (authoring checklist — no renderer change)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Before outputting a model, mentally check it from 4 views:
  FRONT  — width-to-height ratio correct? shoulders wider than hips
           (for robots/humanoids)? head sized correctly vs. body?
  SIDE   — depth matches the reference? any hunch/lean visible if the
           subject has one? do arms extend forward/back correctly?
  TOP    — shoulders/arms at correct X positions? body depth vs. width
           correct?
  3/4    — this is usually the reference image's own angle — does the
           overall silhouette actually match it?

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.10 — SECONDARY DETAIL SYSTEM (authoring checklist — no renderer change)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Small objects that break up large flat surfaces. RULE: any single
surface larger than 0.4×0.4 units MUST get at least one of these:

  PANEL LINES  thin flat boxes (h: 0.01–0.02), parent color darkened
               20%, spanning most of the parent surface's width.
  BOLTS/RIVETS tiny spheres or cylinders (scale 0.03–0.06) at panel
               corners/edges, slightly lighter or darker than parent.
  COLOR TRIM   thin flat boxes along an edge, in an accent color (e.g.
               a green stripe on grey armor).
  VENTS/GRILLES flat boxes (d: 0.02–0.04) recessed slightly into the
               surface, very dark (#111111–#222222). Add 2–5 on large
               flat surfaces like torso fronts.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.11 — COMPLETE EXAMPLE: every v6.3 system used together
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A heavy combat robot using proportions, contactShadow, asymmetry,
skeleton+joint placement, smoothing, colorVariation, toneVariant,
overlap, and secondary detail (vents + chest trim) all at once:

CORRECTED v7.3 — two notes on fields used below:
  - "asymmetry" is now LIVE (see PART 34.7): "shoulder_l_drop"/"head_tilt"
    are read automatically and applied to blueprint.skeleton/rotation for
    any object riding that joint. Because of this, the skeleton below now
    authors shoulder_l/shoulder_r at the SAME y (symmetric baseline) and
    lets asymmetry.shoulder_l_drop apply the actual 0.03 offset — and the
    head object no longer carries its own manual "rotation":[0,0,3],
    since asymmetry.head_tilt now supplies that automatically. (An earlier
    version of this example hand-baked both numbers AND declared them in
    "asymmetry" — they'd disagreed with each other in the shoulder case
    and would have double-applied in the head case once asymmetry went
    live. Author asymmetry in ONE place — either the skeleton/rotation
    fields by hand, OR the "asymmetry" block — never both.)
  - "proportions" remains INFO-ONLY (renders in the Inspector side panel
    for a human to read; does not resize anything). The actual sizing
    below still comes entirely from "skeleton" coordinates and each
    object's own "params"/"scale" — match those to the proportions block
    by hand, the same as before.

{
  "meta": { "name": "Heavy Combat Robot", "description": "Wide hunched mech, gorilla stance" },

      "position": [0, 1.26, 0.18],
      "scale": [0.26, 0.07, 0.04],
      "material": "flat", "baseColor": "#110011",
      "emissive": "#ff44ff", "emissiveIntensity": 2.2
    },
    {

      "joint": "foot_l",
      "params": { "w": 0.28, "h": 0.12, "d": 0.32, "nx": 2, "ny": 2, "nz": 2 },
      "smoothing": "flat",
      "toneVariant": "light",
      "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#c8d0c4"
    },
    {
      "id": "foot_r",
      "type": "generated", "generator": "box_subdivided",
      "joint": "foot_r",
      "params": { "w": 0.28, "h": 0.12, "d": 0.32, "nx": 2, "ny": 2, "nz": 2 },
      "smoothing": "flat",
      "toneVariant": "light",
      "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#c8d0c4"
    },
    {
      "id": "torso_vent_l",
      "type": "cube",
      "position": [0.28, 0.58, 0.24],
      "scale": [0.10, 0.03, 0.06],
      "material": "flat", "baseColor": "#111111"
    },
    {
      "id": "torso_vent_r",
      "type": "cube",
      "position": [-0.28, 0.58, 0.24],
      "scale": [0.10, 0.03, 0.06],
      "material": "flat", "baseColor": "#111111"
    },
    {
      "id": "chest_trim",
      "type": "cube",
      "position": [0, 0.72, 0.24],
      "scale": [0.60, 0.025, 0.04],
      "material": "flat", "baseColor": "#4a7a48"
    }
  ]
}


--------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.12 — GENERATION MODE (authoring convention — no renderer geometry
        effect; added RJS UPDATE 2, N5)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
An optional blueprint-level "meta.generationMode" field documents how
the blueprint was produced:

  "meta": { "generationMode": "no-api" | "api" | "manual" | "hybrid", ... }

  "no-api" — pasted from a chat AI, no API key used (the normal LBL
             workflow: paste this spec + your prompt into your own AI
             chat, then drag the returned blueprint into the viewer)
  "api"    — generated via a hosted API service
  "manual" — hand-authored, no AI involvement
  "hybrid" — partial AI generation plus manual edits

This field is purely informational. The renderer reads it after parsing
and, if the value is "api", shows a small "🔑 API-generated" chip in the
topbar — a passive label only; the renderer never makes an API call
itself regardless of this value. If the field is absent, no badge is
shown and nothing else changes. This complements the topbar's separate,
always-on "Mode: 🔒 No-API" chip, which describes the renderer/viewer
itself (always No-API) rather than any individual blueprint.

================================================================================
PART 28 — PALETTE / COMMENT / AUTOGROUND QUICK REFERENCE (v6.5)
================================================================================
  Palette:   "palette": { "body":"#2a1a3e", "trim":"#c8c8c8" }
             → any "$body" string in objects becomes "#2a1a3e"
  Comments:  // any line starting with // outside a string is stripped
  autoGround:"autoGround": false  ← only to DISABLE the default Y=0 grounding

================================================================================
PART 29 — AI OUTPUT QUALITY RULES (read before generating any blueprint)
================================================================================
These rules are for the AI generating blueprints — not enforced by the renderer,
but violations consistently produce models that look wrong or fail to represent
the subject legibly. Run through these mentally before writing any object.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.1 — COLOR DISCIPLINE (especially critical when sampling from images)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Never use pure #000000 (invisible) or #ffffff (blown out) on any large surface.
  Darkest acceptable:   #1a1a1a
  Lightest acceptable:  #f0f0f0
When sampling dark colors from a reference image, bump them up 10–15% in
luminance so surfaces remain readable in 3D lighting. Adjacent parts must
differ by at least #222222 in luminance to read as separate forms.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.2 — SCALE DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Full model must fit in a 3×3×3 unit bounding box. Ground is Y=0.
  Human character:   height ~1.8 units
  Car / vehicle:     length ~2.0, height ~0.7
  Locomotive:        length ~3.0, height ~1.4
Measure the reference image: what fraction of the total height is the head?
The wheels? Each panel? Use those ratios to set object scales, not guesses.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.3 — POSITION DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
After deciding scale: check every object. Is it underground? Floating?
Do mirrored pairs (l/r) actually mirror at ±X? Objects with attachTo must
NOT also have an explicit "position" field (position wins — see PART 7).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.4 — PART COUNT DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Simple object (cup, chair):         5–15 parts
  Medium object (car, animal):        20–45 parts
  Complex object (locomotive, robot): 50–90 parts
Use radialRepeat/linearArray for repetitive geometry. Use generators for
curved/organic shapes. Never stack cubes to approximate a generator shape.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.5 — MATERIAL DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Match material preset to the physical surface visible in the image:
  Lacquered car body → paint / glossypaint
  Chrome trim        → chrome
  Weathered metal    → rust
  Glowing display    → emissivepanel
  Rubber tyre        → rubber
  Organic surface    → fur / matte / rough
Never use "default" on prominent visible parts. Always set baseColor.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.6 — GENERATOR SELECTION DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Before writing any object: "Is there a generator for this shape?"
  Spoked wheel  → spoked_wheel  (not stacked cylinders + boxes)
  Smoke plume   → smoke_trail   (not 8 manual spheres)
  Curved limb   → tube          (not a chain of scaled cubes)
  Vase/torso    → lathe
  Ear/fin/wing  → extrude
Generator output is geometrically correct; hand-built approximations almost
always have seams, gaps, or wrong scale.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.7 — CONSTRAINT DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Any 2+ objects identical except for position/rotation → radialRepeat or
  linearArray instead.
"leg_l" and "leg_r" as separate objects → "symmetrical" on leg_l, delete leg_r.
3+ siblings evenly spaced → "equalSpacing" constraint.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.8 — OUTPUT FORMAT DISCIPLINE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Always output a complete, valid .js script that builds and returns a
complete blueprint object (not a fragment) — see PART 32. Always include,
on the blueprint object:
  "meta": { "name": "...", "description": "..." }
  "lightingPreset": "studio" | "outdoor" | "night" | "flat"
    (use "outdoor" for daytime exteriors; "night" for dark scenes)
  "palette" block if any color appears 3+ times
  // comments before every major section (// === CHASSIS ===, etc.)
  autoGround: true (default, omit) for ground-contact models
Never output: markdown fences, text before/after the .js code, a file
  that starts with "{" or "[" as its first character (PART 32.2), or a
  script that never returns/assigns the finished "blueprint" object.


================================================================================
================================================================================
PART 23 — CHANGELOG / CORRECTIONS FROM v5.3 + lbl_reference -> v6
================================================================================
This section exists for transparency about what changed and why, so a
future audit can tell what was deliberate.

1. [CRITICAL FIX] Low_Poly_v5_3_optimized.txt's Section 1B ("FORMAT A —
   FLAT objects array, PREFERRED") and Section 12 (color/material
   examples) both showed flat-format objects with "material" as an
   OBJECT — e.g. {"material": {"baseColor": "#c8763a"}}. This is WRONG
   for flat format and will throw inside buildMaterial() (it calls
   obj.material.toLowerCase(), which only works on a string), because
   flat-format objects are never run through the nested-tree material
   -unpacking converter. lbl_reference.txt's convention — "material" as
   a STRING preset name plus a separate top-level "baseColor" — is the
   one that actually matches the renderer, and is what v6 uses
   throughout (PART 1, PART 3, PART 5, PART 22). The object form for
   "material" is only safe in genuinely NESTED-format JSON.

2. [MERGED] lbl_reference.txt and Low_Poly_v5_3_optimized.txt were
   ~70% overlapping content (same primitives/materials/operations/
   constraints/placement/CSG/extrude/lathe/sdf/vertexpaint/decimate/
   textures/generate/phyllotaxis/variation sections, described twice in
   slightly different words). v6 keeps one copy of each, verified
   against code, instead of two.

3. [PRESERVED, CONDENSED] The qualitative modeling guidance unique to
   Low_Poly_v5_3_optimized.txt — Master Detail Directive, reference
   -image input mode, proportion system, semantic traits, generation
   thinking process — is not enforced by any code path (it's pure prompt
   engineering for the AI generating the JSON, not a renderer feature),
   but is high-value, so it's preserved in full in PART 20 rather than
   dropped just because it isn't "verifiable."

4. [NEW] PART 19 (Nested Tree Format Deep Dive) did not exist as a
   standalone section in either source document. The exact rule for
   when a nested child's "position" is world-space vs. a local
   attachTo-style offset (parentHasDeformOp / parentIsSmallSubFeature in
   flattenTree()) was previously undocumented entirely — both old docs
   only said positions are "body-relative" without describing the two
   exceptions. This is very likely the root cause of any nested-format
   model rendering with disconnected/floating parts.

5. [NEW] PART 4's explicit-position-overrides-attachTo precedence rule
   (solvePosition() returns immediately if obj.position is set, ignoring
   attachTo/placement/anchor entirely) was not stated as a hard rule in
   either source document — Section 10's notes implied offset/anchor
   compose with placement, without flagging that a literal "position"
   field bypasses the whole system. Flagged prominently in PART 1, PART
   4, and PART 21.

6. [CLARIFIED] PART 16 (generate macro): expression evaluation actually
   runs through a real JS Function() body, so it supports more than the
   documented +,-,*,/,ternary subset (e.g. Math.sin works). Noted as a
   bonus capability, while still recommending the documented subset for
   forward compatibility.

7. [CLARIFIED] PART 17 (phyllotaxis): multiple template children cycle
   via index modulo, rather than only the first child ever being used —
   not previously stated. Also clarified that phyllotaxis children use
   the flat-style "material" string convention, not the nested-format
   object convention, matching the lbl_reference.txt example (which was
   correct) rather than treating it as ambiguous.

8. [VERIFIED, NO CHANGE NEEDED] Every primitive type, material preset,
   operation, constraint, and placement keyword listed in PART 2/3/8/9/7
   was individually checked against a real `case` (or equivalent
   explicit handling) in the renderer's source. None were found to be
   purely aspirational/undocumented-in-code. If a future version of the
   renderer adds or removes any of these, this document will need a
   re-audit — it is accurate as of the file inspected on 2026-06-26, not
   guaranteed accurate forever.

9. [NEW, v6.3] Added PART 26 — Advanced Model Quality System, ten
   upgrades aimed at the gap between "geometrically correct" and
   "actually looks hand-crafted":
     - Renderer features (real code, verified against buildScene() /
       buildMaterial() / buildGeometry() / finalizeSceneBuild()):
       skeleton joint placement + toggleable joint markers (26.1),
       smoothing groups flat/smooth/auto/custom-angle (26.3), per-face
       color variation (26.4), part overlap/padding (26.5), contact
       shadow (26.6), material tone variant (26.7). Smoothing and color
       variation are also live-editable from the Inspector panel
       (dropdown + slider) without re-pasting JSON.
     - UI-only display: proportion lock (26.2) — shown in the Inspector
       panel, never auto-corrects geometry.
     - Pure JSON-authoring conventions, no renderer change: asymmetry
       (26.8), silhouette quality checklist (26.9), secondary detail
       rule (26.10).

10. [FIXED, v6.3.1] PART 25's generator dispatch silently fell back to a
    default-sized sphere — completely ignoring "scale" — for any
    unrecognized "generator" name, with no recovery path. The single
    most common real-world casualty: "generator":"cylinder", the most
    natural-sounding name for a boiler/limb/barrel, doesn't exist in
    v6.2/v6.3 and silently became a ~1-unit sphere every time, "scale"
    and all. Fixed two ways:
      - Added three new real generators that didn't exist before:
        "cylinder" (alias "cyl"), "cone", "capsule" — each mirroring its
        base-primitive namesake's exact sizing convention, plus an
        independent radiusTop/radiusBottom option for cylinder that the
        base primitive can't express.
      - Hardened sphere/disc/box_subdivided/extrude so that when their
        own size params (rx/ry/rz, w/h/d, depth, etc.) are omitted, they
        now fall back to "scale" instead of a flat hardcoded default —
        verified with a standalone THREE.js bounding-box test, not just
        a syntax check. "params" is still recommended for precision; this
        only changes what happens when it's missing.
    See PART 25's new callout for the complete, current set.

11. [NEW, v6.4] Eight renderer and spec upgrades (verified 2026-06-30):

    a. HemisphereLight added to the scene (sky #B4C8E8, ground #6B5A3E,
       intensity 0.6). Immediately makes all models look grounded —
       undersides get warm ground-bounce, tops get sky colour.

    b. Lighting presets (toolbar "💡" button): Studio (default), Outdoor
       (stronger sun + blue sky + warm fog), Night (dim + cool fill +
       stronger fog), Flat (ambient only). Documented as optional
       "lightingPreset" key at blueprint level.

    c. radialRepeat constraint (PART 8): object-style constraint that
       clones a mesh N times rotating around a local axis. Purely visual
       — never in the scene tree, never exported. Ideal for spokes,
       bolt rings, petals, gear teeth visible in reference images.

    d. linearArray constraint (PART 8): object-style constraint that
       clones a mesh N times at regular spacing along an axis. Ideal for
       track ties, fence posts, window rows visible in reference images.

    e. Toon outline (toolbar "🖊 Outline" button): back-face-expand
       method. Works best with "smoothing":"smooth" on rounded parts.
       Note in spec: smooth-shaded parts get cleaner outlines.

    f. spoked_wheel generator (PART 25): rim ring + hub + N spokes
       merged into one BufferGeometry. Y-up by default; rotation:[90,0,0]
       for sideways wheel.

    g. smoke_trail generator (PART 25): arc of spheres sampled along a
       quadratic Bezier curve, merged into one BufferGeometry 
       vertex-colour opacity baking. Replaces 6–10 manual smoke spheres.

    h. 8 new material presets (PART 3): paint, glossypaint, chrome, rust,
       emissivepanel, ceramic, rubber, wood. Aliases: glossy_paint→
       glossypaint, emissive_panel→emissivepanel.

    i. Rotation axis cheat sheet added to PART 2 — prevents the most
       common mis-orientation bug (cylinder/cone/capsule are Y-up by
       default; use rotation to repoint them).

12. [NEW, v6.5] Eight systemic improvements (renderer + spec):

    a. Comment stripping: state-machine pre-parser strips // single-line
       comments before JSON.parse. Spec "no comments" rule reversed; AI
       encouraged to annotate every section with // labels. /* */ not
       supported.

    b. Palette system: applyPalette() pre-pass substitutes "$varName"
       string values from blueprint.palette before buildScene(). Palette
       block documented in PART 1; PART 29 checklist requires it for 3+
       repeated colors.

    c. smoke_trail position fix: spec clarified — startPos/endPos are LOCAL
       OFFSETS, not world coords. Old wrong examples replaced with correct
       position+startPos:[0,0,0] pattern. No renderer change needed.

    d. smoke_trail color fix: removed vertex-color baking from
       genSmokeTrail(). The "color" param now propagates to obj.baseColor
       and "startOpacity" to obj.opacity before buildMaterial() runs.
       Vertex-color approach caused wrong colors due to missing
       vertexColors:true on the material. Spec updated.

    e. Lighting improvement: hemiLight 0.6→0.8, fillLight 0.6→1.0. New
       back-left rimBL DirectionalLight (0xaaccff, 0.8) added — separates
       dark models from background. All LIGHTING_PRESETS updated. DARK
       COLOR WARNING added to PART 3 with image-sampling guidance.

    f. tube path WARNING: prominent ⚠ box added to tube generator section
       with wrong/correct examples. PART 21 checklist item added.

    g. autoGround flag: finalizeSceneBuild() checks
       CURRENT_BLUEPRINT.autoGround !== false before applying Y=0 shift.
       Default true (existing behavior preserved). Spec PART 1 + checklist.

    h. PART 29 — AI OUTPUT QUALITY RULES: eight sections covering color,
       scale, position, part count, material, generator, constraint, and
       output format discipline. Spec-only, no renderer change.

13. [NEW, v6.6] Nine renderer upgrades — performance, image-based lighting,
    a new generator family, and real schema validation. Identical to
    Prompt_To_Json's v6.6 changelog entry 13 — see PART 30 below for full
    field documentation (unchanged whether the source was a prompt or a
    reference image, same as everything else in this fork).

    a. Auto-instancing for array/scatter operations (THREE.InstancedMesh
       once instance count reaches 6 — automatic, no JSON change).
    b. Level of Detail: new optional per-object "lod" field.
    c. SSAO toggle ("🌑 AO" toolbar button) — viewer setting, not JSON.
    d. Procedural HDRI/PMREM environment lighting ("🌐 HDRI" toolbar
       button, RoomEnvironment-based, no external file) — viewer setting.
    e. Auto-scaling: new optional blueprint-level "autoScale" field.
    f. L-system / grammar generator: "generator":"lsystem" (aliases
       "l_system", "grammar") — turtle-graphics branching structures.
    g. Bounding-volume precomputation on every generated geometry —
       performance only, no behavior change.
    h. JSON Schema validation: validateBlueprintSchema() — real
       structural/enum checking, replacing the old minimal id/type check.
    i. Live perf HUD chip (renderer.info triangle/draw-call counts).

END OF v6.5 IMAGE-TO-JS SPECIFICATION — see PART 30 below for v6.6 additions
This fork adds PART 27 (IMAGE-TO-JS ANALYSIS PROTOCOL) near the top.
If you started reading from the bottom: go back to PART 27 before generating.

================================================================================
PART 30 — v6.6 ADDITIONS (performance, IBL, L-systems, schema validation)
================================================================================
Reproduced unchanged from the master spec — see PART 27 for how to ground
any of these fields (autoScale, lod, lsystem params) in actual measurements
from a reference image rather than inventing them from semantic priors.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.1 — AUTO-INSTANCING (array/scatter operations, PART 9-15)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
No JSON changes. "lineararray"/"gridarray"/"radialarray"/"scatter"
operations you already write work exactly as before; the renderer just
silently uses one THREE.InstancedMesh instead of N clones once the
resulting instance count reaches 6. Nothing to author differently.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.2 — LEVEL OF DETAIL: "lod" (per-object, optional)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "lod": {
    "levels": [
      { "distance": 8,  "detail": 0.5 },
      { "distance": 20, "detail": 0.15 }
    ]
  }
"distance" = camera distance (world units) at which this level kicks in.
"detail" = fraction of original vertices to KEEP. Only use this on
genuinely high-vertex objects (SDF blobs, marching-cubes output, dense
subdivision) that will actually be viewed from a range of distances.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.3 — SSAO / HDRI (viewer settings, not JSON fields)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Toolbar toggles ("🌑 AO", "🌐 HDRI") — nothing to author in JSON.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.4 — AUTO-SCALING: "autoScale" (blueprint-level, optional)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "autoScale": true      // normalize tallest dimension to 1 unit
  "autoScale": 2.5        // normalize tallest dimension to 2.5 units
Applied after generation, before "autoGround" grounding and camera
framing. Especially useful in the image-to-JSON workflow when several
models are built from reference images shot at different distances/
focal lengths and need to end up at a consistent world-space scale.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.5 — L-SYSTEM / GRAMMAR GENERATOR: "generator":"lsystem"
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  {
    "id": "tree_branch_01",
    "generator": "lsystem",   // aliases: "l_system", "grammar"
    "params": {
      "axiom": "F",
      "rules": { "F": "F[+F]F[-F]F" },
      "iterations": 3,
      "angle": 25,
      "length": 0.35,
      "lengthFalloff": 0.82,
      "radius": 0.05,
      "radiusFalloff": 0.72,
      "segments": 5
    },
    "material": "wood",
    "baseColor": "#5a4530"
  }
Turtle alphabet: F=forward+draw, f=forward only, +/-=turn left/right,
&/^=pitch down/up, \/=roll, |=180° turn, [/]=push/pop state (tapering
length/radius per lengthFalloff/radiusFalloff). Any other character is a
grammar placeholder (no geometry) — useful for structuring rules.
For image-grounded branching subjects (a tree/coral/root system visible
in the reference), measure the REAL branching angle and generation count
from the image (PART 27.2's proportion-measurement approach) rather than
defaulting to the 25°/3-iteration example above — a windswept pine reads
very differently from a symmetrical ornamental shrub, and that difference
is exactly what "angle" and "rules" are for.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.6 — SCHEMA VALIDATION (informational — the renderer does this for you)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Malformed JSON (wrong field types, duplicate ids, non-array "objects")
now surfaces as a clear validation-log message instead of a silent bad
render. Unknown-but-gracefully-handled names (e.g. an unknown material)
are warnings, don't block the build.

================================================================================
END OF v6.6 IMAGE-TO-JS SPECIFICATION — see PART 31 below for v6.7 additions
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 31 — v6.7 ADDITIONS (kitbash part library, trim bands, param tuning)
================================================================================
Reproduced unchanged from the master spec. Plus: colorVariation now
defaults to OFF — see PART 26.4 above (already updated in place).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.1 — KITBASH / MODULAR PART LIBRARY: "partRef" (per-object)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "id": "wheel_fl", "partRef": "wheel_assembly", "position": [0.9,0.3,1.4] }

A localStorage-backed part library, populated by the human via the
Inspector's "💾 Save as Part" button or the "📦 Parts" toolbar panel. This
is HUMAN-driven — don't invent a partRef name the human hasn't told you
exists. Any field on the SAME object (position/rotation/scale/material/
color/id) overrides the saved part's corresponding field; everything else
(generator, params, operations, constraints) carries through unchanged.
Useful in the image-to-json workflow for recurring parts across multiple
reference-image-based builds (e.g. the same bolt/hinge/wheel design
appearing in several photographed products).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.2 — TRIM BAND / STRIPE / BELT GENERATOR: "generator":"trimband"
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  {
    "id": "chest_trim",
    "generator": "trimband",           // aliases: "trim_band", "band"
    "params": {
      "radius": 0.42, "width": 0.05, "segs": 16,
      "phiStart": 0, "phiLength": 360
    },
    "position": [0, 0.8, 0],
    "material": "metal", "baseColor": "#c93a3a"
  }
Hollow shell (open-ended cylinder) wrapped around a parent shape — for
racing stripes, armor seams, belt lines, collar/cuff bands, pipe
collars. When the reference image shows a visible stripe or trim line
that doesn't cleanly wrap 360° (e.g. a stripe only down the front), use
"phiLength" below 360 and set "phiStart" to the angle where it begins —
measure this from the image the same way PART 27.2 measures proportions,
don't guess a default.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.3 — GENERATOR PARAMETER TUNING (informational — the renderer checks this)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
validateBlueprintSchema() now checks known generator params against sane
ranges and flags operation counts over 500 as a probable typo. Surfaces
as validation-log warnings — worth checking before output.

================================================================================
PART 32 — v7.0 OUTPUT FORMAT CHANGE: JAVASCRIPT (.js), NOT JSON
================================================================================
Reproduced unchanged from Prompt_To_Js.txt — this is the same output-format
contract for both forks, image-grounded or prompt-only. Everything PART 27
tells you to MEASURE from the reference image is unaffected; this PART only
covers HOW you hand that measured content to the renderer.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.1 — WHY, AND WHAT DID NOT CHANGE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer used to require a literal JSON text file: you hand-typed the
whole "objects" array as static data, and the renderer's file loader ran
it straight through JSON.parse(). The renderer now ALSO (and preferentially,
for AI-generated output) accepts a JavaScript (.js) file: a real script
that, when executed, produces that exact same object. Nothing about PARTs
1-31 changed — every primitive, material, operation, constraint, and
generator; the flat-vs-nested top-level shape rule (PART 1); the
attachTo/joint/position rules; all of it is identical. The only thing
that's different is that you're now writing CODE that builds the object,
not the object's text representation directly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.2 — THE CONTRACT THE RENDERER ENFORCES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer decides whether an uploaded/pasted file is JSON or JS by one
rule: if the trimmed content starts with "{" or "[", it's parsed as JSON;
otherwise it's executed as JavaScript. Since your output is now always a
.js script, NEVER start the file with "{" — start with a comment, or a
"const"/"let"/"function" statement, as real JS naturally does.
The script is run as a function body (no access to the page's other
variables, no DOM, no network — treat it as a pure, self-contained
computation). It must leave the renderer with a finished blueprint object,
via ONE of:
  (a) an explicit `return <object>;` or `return blueprint;` as the last
      statement actually reached by execution, OR
  (b) a top-level `const blueprint = {...};` (or let/var) — if execution
      finishes without hitting a return, the runner reads this variable
      automatically.
Prefer (a) with a plain `return blueprint;` at the very end. The
returned/assigned object must be EXACTLY the same shape PART 1 describes:
either flat `{ meta:{...}, objects:[...] }` or nested `{ type:'group',
children:[...] }` (or `{ name:..., type:'group', children:[...] }`).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.3 — WHAT "USE REAL JAVASCRIPT" MEANS IN PRACTICE (IMAGE-GROUNDED CASE)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
For image-to-JS specifically, real code pays off most in exactly the
places PART 27 already asks you to do repetitive measured work:
  - A radialArray-style loop for scales/feathers/rivets/window panes
    counted directly off the reference, where the loop's iteration count
    IS the measured count (27.2) rather than an eyeballed generic number.
  - A shared helper function for bilaterally symmetric parts whose
    measured proportions came from ONE visible side of the image (27.6) —
    build it once, call it twice with the appropriate mirroring.
