  - Plain variables for a color sampled once (27.4) and reused across
    every part of that material region, so the sampled hex only needs to
    be transcribed correctly in one place.
  - Simple math translating a measured pixel ratio or angle (27.2/27.3)
    straight into a scale/rotation value, instead of pre-computing it by
    hand.
  Don't force loops/helpers onto a reference that's genuinely a small
  handful of distinct, non-repeating parts — plain object literals for
  "objects" are still correct there.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.4 — JS-SPECIFIC PRE-OUTPUT CHECKLIST (in addition to PART 21)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  [ ] File does NOT start with "{" or "[" as its very first character.
  [ ] Output is pure JavaScript only — no markdown fences, no JSON text
      embedded as a string anywhere, no explanation before/after the code.
  [ ] Script ends with `return blueprint;` (or `return {...}`) OR defines
      a top-level `blueprint` variable if you intentionally never return.
  [ ] The final object matches PART 1's shape exactly — running it
      through JS didn't relax anything PART 21 (or PART 27.8) checks.
  [ ] No DOM access, no network calls, no `import`/`require`.
  [ ] If you used loops/helpers for a measured repeated pattern, spot-
      check the generated count/positions against the actual measurement
      from 27.2 (an off-by-one loop bound is now possible in a way it
      wasn't with static JSON).

================================================================================
PART 33 — v7.1 ADDITIONS (the 19-technique modeling/positioning pass)
================================================================================
This section documents the changes made against a specific, named checklist
of 19 "3D Modeling Techniques for Better Models + Positioning" the human
requested. Each item below is marked exactly one of:
  [NEW]        — didn't exist in the renderer before v7.1, added now.
  [ENHANCED]   — existed before, v7.1 extends it.
  [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED] — the technique was already fully present; v7.1
                 changed nothing, this is a corrected/confirmed cross-
                 reference so you stop re-deriving it from scratch.
  [ASPIRATIONAL / NOT IMPLEMENTED] — not in the code; do not use.
Verified line-by-line against low_poly_3d.html on 2026-07-04.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.1 — CORE GEOMETRY TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

#1 Advanced Procedural Generators — [NEW] "generator":"voronoi" |
"superellipsoid" | "fractal" | "text3d"

  { "id": "crystal", "generator": "voronoi",
    "params": { "seedCount": 16, "shape": "sphere", "jitter": 0.35, "radius": 0.5 } }
Shatters a base sphere/box into faceted Voronoi-cell chunks (crystal
clusters, turtle shells, cracked rock). "shape": "sphere"|"box".

  { "id": "pillow", "generator": "superellipsoid",
    "params": { "n1": 2.5, "n2": 2.5, "rx": 0.5, "ry": 0.3, "rz": 0.4 } }
One continuous family spanning sphere (n=2) → cube (n large) → rounded
pillow (n<2) → pinched/star form (n<1). "n1" = north-south exponent,
"n2" = east-west exponent, independently tunable.

  { "id": "asteroid", "generator": "fractal",
    "params": { "iterations": 3, "roughness": 0.4, "seed": 7, "radius": 0.6 } }
Recursive midpoint-style displacement of an icosphere — each pass adds
finer, weaker noise (amplitude halves per iteration). Same "seed" always
reproduces the same rock. For asteroids, alien terrain, cratered moons,
gnarled bark knots.

  { "id": "sign", "generator": "text3d",
    "params": { "text": "AI", "resolution": 14, "depth": 0.15 } }
Extruded LOW-POLY BLOCK text — NOT smooth-curved typography. Works 
zero network access (this renderer has none): it rasterizes the string
to an offscreen canvas and extrudes every "ink" cell into its own box, a
deliberately voxel/blocky typeface that matches this renderer's whole
aesthetic. "resolution" = grid cells per character (6-28, higher = more
legible but heavier). "text" capped at 24 characters. Don't ask for this
generator expecting smooth vector letterforms — it will not produce them.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#2 Combined Deformers — [ENHANCED] single-pass "deform" operation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Twist, bend, and taper already existed as three separate "operations"
array entries (each fully functional on its own — see PART 9-15) — an
author could already chain all three on one object. v7.1 adds a genuine
single-pass combinator that walks the vertex buffer ONCE and recomputes
normals ONCE, instead of three separate walks + three normal recomputes:

  { "operations": [
    { "type": "deform", "params": {
        "twistAngle": 45, "bendAngle": 20, "bendAxis": "x",
        "taperAmount": 0.3, "taperAxis": "y"
    }}
  ]}
All params optional/independent — omit any of twistAngle/bendAngle/
taperAmount to skip that part of the deform. Order applied internally:
twist → taper → bend (matches the order the three separate ops already
produce when chained in that sequence). Prefer this over three separate
ops when you want all three on one object; the three separate ops still
work exactly as before for partial/staged deforms.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#3 Displacement with Normal/Noise — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
"texturePattern" + "normalStrength" + "displacementAmount" already existed
(buildMaterial() generates a matched {map, normalMap, displacementMap}
trio procedurally, no external texture files, no UV-authoring needed
beyond what every primitive already gets). See #6 below for the pattern
name list, now extended.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#4 Clean Boolean CSG — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
"operations": [{"type":"union"|"difference"|"intersect", "params":{"with":"otherId"}}]
Real BVH-accelerated CSG (three-bvh-csg, lazy-loaded on first use).
Resolved in a deferred pass after every mesh exists, so the "with" target
can appear anywhere in the objects array. Unchanged in v7.1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#5 Automatic LOD Generation — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  "lod": { "levels": [ {"distance":8,"detail":0.5}, {"distance":20,"detail":0.15} ] }
Per-object, optional. "detail" = fraction of original vertices kept past
that camera distance. Author-specified reduction fractions, not an
automatic target-framerate solver — use on genuinely high-vertex objects
(SDF blobs, dense fractal/voronoi output) viewed across a range of
distances. Unchanged in v7.1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.2 — MATERIAL & SURFACE TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

#6 Procedural Texture Patterns — [ENHANCED]
"texturePattern": "wood" | "wood_grain" | "stone" | "stone_cracks" |
                  "brick" | "scales" | "fabric" | "fabric_weave" |
                  "metal_brushed" (new)
wood/stone/brick/scales/fabric already existed; v7.1 adds:
  - "metal_brushed" (aliases "metalbrushed","brushedmetal","metal") —
    directional streaks + fine grain, for brushed aluminum/stainless.
    Pair with material:"metal"/"chrome" and low roughness.
  - "stone_cracks" (aliases "cracks","crackle") — thin dark fracture
    lines along jittered Voronoi-cell edges, for cracked earth/dried mud/
    weathered rock — visually distinct from the pre-existing speckled
    "stone" noise pattern.
  - "wood_grain" and "fabric_weave" are now accepted as explicit name
    aliases for the pre-existing "wood" and "fabric" patterns (same
    output, just the more literal name from this technique list also works).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#7 PBR Extensions — [NEW] clearcoat, sheen, anisotropy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "material": "paint", "clearcoat": 0.8, "clearcoatRoughness": 0.1 }
  { "material": "fabric", "sheen": 0.6, "sheenColor": "#ffddaa", "sheenRoughness": 0.5 }
  { "material": "metal", "anisotropy": 0.7, "anisotropyRotation": 90 }
Setting ANY of "clearcoat"/"sheen"/"anisotropy" switches that object's
material from MeshStandardMaterial to MeshPhysicalMaterial automatically
— objects that don't set these fields are completely unaffected (still
the cheaper MeshStandardMaterial, zero behavior change). All three are
independent and combinable on the same object.
  clearcoat (0-1): lacquer/varnish layer — car paint, glossy plastic,
    nail polish, wet-look surfaces.
  sheen (0-1): soft grazing-angle backscatter — velvet, brushed fabric,
    peach-fuzz surfaces. sheenColor defaults to the object's own base color.
  anisotropy (0-1): stretches specular highlights along one tangent
    direction — brushed metal, hair, vinyl records, CDs. anisotropyRotation
    is in DEGREES (0 = along the surface's U tangent).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#8 Vertex Color Variation — [ENHANCED] gradients + noise per face
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Per-face random noise via "colorVariation" (0-0.25) already existed.
v7.1 adds an optional directional gradient layered underneath it:
  { "colorVariation": 0.08,
    "colorVariationGradient": { "axis": "y", "from": "#223311", "to": "#88cc55" } }
Each face's base color is now: lerp(from, to, faceHeightOnAxis) BEFORE
the existing per-face random noise offset is added — reads as "darker at
the root, lighter at the tip" (feathers, horns, foliage, roots) instead
of pure uniform noise. Omit "colorVariationGradient" and behavior is
identical to before — fully backward compatible.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.3 — STRUCTURE TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

#9 Bone-Based Skinning — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
GLB export already builds a REAL rig, not just joint markers: every
mesh becomes a THREE.SkinnedMesh bound 100% to its own THREE.Bone
(rigid single-bone skin weight — the simplest possible VALID skin, not
a stub). Bone parent-child comes from each part's attachTo/parent chain.
Open the exported GLB in Blender: "hand" really is a child bone of
"forearm" really is a child of "upper_arm", fully selectable/rotatable.
The live in-browser viewer still shows plain nested meshes (visually
identical either way) — this only changes what the exported GLB IS.
Unchanged in v7.1 except for the auto-rig fallback in #10 below.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#10 Auto-Rigging — [ENHANCED] nearest-joint-above heuristic
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Previously, bone parent-child ONLY came from an object's own explicit
attachTo/parent field — a part riding a named "joint" (blueprint.skeleton)
with no attachTo got exported as an unparented root bone. v7.1 adds a
fallback: when blueprint.skeleton exists, the renderer auto-infers a
parent for every joint using "nearest OTHER joint that sits above it"
(the same first-pass heuristic real auto-rig tools use, requires no
naming convention) — then any joint-riding part missing an explicit
attachTo bones to whichever OTHER part rides that inferred parent joint,
instead of falling back to root. This is heuristic joint-graph inference
over an ALREADY-AUTHORED skeleton coordinate map — it does not detect
limbs from raw unrigged geometry with no skeleton data at all; still
author (or have the AI author) blueprint.skeleton for anything you want
auto-rigged. No new JSON fields — this activates automatically whenever
blueprint.skeleton is present and a part is missing attachTo.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#11 Instancing + Smart Grouping — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Any array/scatter operation (PART 9-15) whose resulting instance count
reaches the instancing threshold automatically renders as one
THREE.InstancedMesh instead of N clones — nothing to author differently,
purely a renderer-side performance optimization. Unchanged in v7.1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#12 Phyllotaxis + Arrays — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED] / angle param [NEW]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Phyllotaxis groups (golden-angle nature-scatter distribution) already
existed and are unchanged. Radial array gets one new param in v7.1:
  { "type": "radialarray", "params": { "count": 5, "radius": 1.2, "angle": 120 } }
"angle" = total sweep in degrees. Default 360 (full ring, identical to
prior behavior) — a value below 360 spreads "count" copies evenly across
a partial ARC instead (e.g. a 120° fan of feathers rather than a full
circle of them).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.4 — POSITIONING TECHNIQUES FOR JSON
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

#13 Constraint-Based Positioning — [NEW] align_to, snap_to_face,
distribute_even, radial_array angle (angle covered in #12 above)

  { "id": "lamp2", "alignTo": { "target": "lamp1", "axes": ["z"], "own": [3,0.5,0] } }
Copies the named world AXES from another already-positioned object while
this object's own coordinates (from "own", default [0,0,0]) hold for the
rest — e.g. "line these lamp posts up on the same Z" without deriving a
full attachTo/placement relationship. Distinct from "attachTo" +
"placement" (which derives the WHOLE position from a target's bounding
box) — alignTo only pins the specific axes you name.

  { "id": "sign", "attachTo": "post", "snapToFace": "left" }
"snapToFace" is a literal-name alias for the SAME face-placement math the
pre-existing "placement" field already used (left/right/above/below/
front/back/etc, PART 8) — added because "snap_to_face" is the more
literal constraint-language name; both fields resolve identically.

  { "operations": [{ "type": "distributeEven",
      "params": { "count": 8, "from": [-3,0,0], "to": [3,0,1.2], "orient": true } }]}
Places "count" evenly-spaced clones between two explicit WORLD POINTS
(not just spacing along one axis from the original's own position, which
is all the pre-existing "lineararray" op does) — for "N fence posts
between these two corners" where the corners aren't a simple single-axis
offset. "orient": true rotates each copy to face along the from→to
direction.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#14 Relative Transform Stack — parent_space/local_offset [ALREADY
IMPLEMENTED as attachTo+offset] / look_at [ENHANCED] / Matrix4 [see note]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
"attachTo"/"parent" + "offset" already IS this renderer's parent-space +
local-offset system (PART 8) — an offset is always resolved in the
target's local frame, not world space. No new fields needed there.

A directional "look_at" vector already existed too, via top-level "aim"
(or the dynamic anchor object's "aim") — { "aim": [0,0,1] } orients the
object's forward axis along a fixed WORLD DIRECTION. v7.1 adds the thing
that was actually missing: looking at ANOTHER OBJECT BY ID (a real
relative relationship, not a fixed vector), resolved in a deferred pass
after every object has its final position (so the target can appear
anywhere in the array):
  { "id": "turret", "lookAt": "player_marker" }
  { "id": "turret2", "lookAt": { "target": "player_marker", "offset": [0,0.3,0] } }

Matrix4 chains: N/A for JSON output — Matrix4 composition is a JS-authoring
technique, not a JSON field. See PART 32 (JS output format) for
how this applies when generating a .js build script instead of static JSON.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#15 Expression-Driven Placement — [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "generate": { "count": 8, "as": "i", "template": {
      "id": "post_{{i}}",
      "position": ["{{ i*0.5 + Math.sin(i) }}", 0, "{{ i*0.3 }}"]
  }}}
The "generate" macro (PART 5.3-era feature) already expands `{{ expr }}`
placeholders against the loop's own index variable (name from "as",
default "i") plus "t" (0-1 normalized progress) BEFORE the rest of the
pipeline ever sees the JSON — this already covers "index * 0.5 + sin(i)"-
style placement exactly as requested. No renderer change needed; this
entry exists so you stop assuming it's missing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#16 Anchor + Pivot Control — anchor [ALREADY IMPLEMENTED] / pivotOffset [NEW]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
"anchor": "bottom_center" (and 9 other named bbox corners/edges — PART 8)
already existed for picking WHERE on a PARENT's bounding box a child
attaches. v7.1 adds "pivotOffset" for the other half of this technique —
which point of the OBJECT'S OWN geometry acts as its local origin, shifted
before position/rotation/scale ever apply:
  { "id": "door", "type": "cube", "scale":[1,2,0.1],
    "pivotOffset": [0.5, 0, 0], "rotation": [0, 40, 0] }
Moves the rotation/scale pivot to the object's own right edge (half its
width on X) — the door now swings from its edge like a hinge instead of
spinning around its center. Purely a geometry-space shift; doesn't move
where the object visually sits unless you also rotate/scale it afterward.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#17 Bounding Box Queries — [NEW] literal bbox_min/bbox_max/center anchor names
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "id": "flag", "attachTo": "pole", "anchor": "bbox_max" }
The 10 named anchors (top_center, bottom_left, etc.) were always backed
by an internal THREE.Box3 query on the target — v7.1 exposes the raw
corners directly under the literal field names this positioning-technique
spec uses: "bbox_min", "bbox_max", "center" (alias "bbox_center"), on top
of the existing named-corner anchors, which are unchanged and still the
more readable choice for anything on-axis (top/bottom/left/right center).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.5 — STYLE TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

#18 Toon/Cel-Shading — [NEW] real stepped-band shading / edge outline
[ALREADY IMPLEMENTED, v6.4]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
"material": "toon" or "cartoon" (both pre-existing preset NAMES) now
route to a genuinely different, non-PBR shading model instead of just a
flat-ish MeshStandardMaterial preset:
  { "material": "toon", "toonBands": 4 }
  { "toonShading": true, "toonBands": 3 }     // works on ANY material name
Uses real THREE.MeshToonMaterial with a small stepped gradient-ramp
texture (NearestFilter, so lighting falls into "toonBands" discrete
bands — 2 to 8, default 4 — instead of smooth PBR falloff). This is a
different material class from clearcoat/sheen/anisotropy (#7) — toon
shading and those PBR extensions are mutually exclusive on the same
object; toon wins if both are requested, since MeshToonMaterial doesn't
support roughness/metalness/clearcoat at all.
Sharp edge outlines were ALREADY implemented since v6.4 — the "🖊 Outline"
toolbar toggle (back-face-expansion method: a scaled-up, normal-flipped
black clone rendered behind each mesh, not literal screen-space edge
detection, but the standard trick for achieving the same visual result
cheaply) — pairs naturally with toon shading for the classic cel-shaded
look but works with any material.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
#19 Wireframe Overlay — [NEW] "◱ Wireframe" toolbar toggle
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Viewer setting, not a JSON field (same category as the pre-existing 🌑 AO
and 🌐 HDRI toggles — nothing to author). Overlays a same-size, non-scaled
green wireframe line mesh on every part in the scene for inspecting
actual triangle topology/poly density — distinct from the outline system
above, which is a stylized scaled-up silhouette, not a topology debug
view. Toggle it independently of Outline/AO/HDRI; all four combine freely.

================================================================================
PART 34 — v7.2 ADDITIONS (fixes to v7.1 features + 8 new techniques)
================================================================================
This is the FOLLOW-UP pass to PART 33's v7.1 work: it fixes six
real gaps left in v7.1 features (PART A below) and adds eight new
techniques that were never part of the original 19 (PART B below). Each
item is marked exactly one of:
  [FIXED]      — a v7.1 feature had a real, working-but-incomplete gap;
                 v7.2 closes it. The v7.1 feature itself still works as
                 documented; this is additive, not a correction of a bug.
  [NEW]        — didn't exist in the renderer before v7.2, added now.
Verified line-by-line against low_poly_3d.html on 2026-07-04.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART A — FIXES TO EXISTING v7.1 FEATURES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

A1. CSG auto-cleanup — [FIXED] runPendingCSG() boolean ops

Boolean union/subtract/intersect (three-bvh-csg) still leaves a denser,
non-uniform mesh right at the seam, same as before — the difference is
you no longer have to remember to chain a manual decimate/remesh/simplify
operation afterward. Immediately after a boolean op resolves, the
renderer now runs THREE's mergeVertices (BufferGeometryUtils) to weld
coincident seam vertices, then runs the existing SimplifyModifier-based
decimate pass at a conservative ~87.5% keep-ratio — UNLESS the object's
own "operations" array already contains an explicit decimate/remesh/
simplify step, in which case auto-cleanup is skipped so the mesh isn't
simplified twice. No new JSON field — this is unconditional behavior on
every "booleanunion"/"boolean_subtract"/etc. operation. If a boolean
result ever looks wrong, the auto-cleanup catches and logs the failure
via console.warn and falls back to the raw (uncleaned) boolean result
rather than breaking the build.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A2. Auto-rig fallback pivot — [FIXED] buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport()
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

v7.1's auto-rigging only produced a correctly-jointed skeleton when
blueprint.skeleton (an explicit {jointName:[x,y,z]} map) was present. A
model built purely from attachTo/parent chains — no skeleton map at all —
still got one bone per part (that part already worked), but every bone's
pivot sat at the child mesh's own local origin, so posing/rotating a bone
in Blender rotated the part around its own middle instead of the joint,
looking like independently-spinning boxes rather than a real limb.

v7.2 adds a second path that only activates when blueprint.skeleton is
ABSENT: any attachTo/parent chain 3 or more parts deep (a genuine limb —
e.g. shoulder→upper_arm→forearm→hand) gets its bones' pivots relocated to
the shared boundary between each part and its parent (the overlap of
their world-space bounding boxes, or the midpoint between their nearest
points if there's a small authored gap) instead of the part's own origin.
The existing explicit-skeleton path is completely unchanged — it never
takes this branch. Nothing to author: this is automatic whenever a model
has no "skeleton" block and a 3+-deep attachTo chain. The live viewer
looks pixel-identical either way; the only difference is what rotates
around what once the exported GLB is posed elsewhere.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A3. Generic bounding-box references — [FIXED] "ref:" resolver
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

v7.1's bbox_min/bbox_max/center queries only worked as the "anchor" value
during attachTo resolution (PART 32 #17). v7.2 lets the same query appear
anywhere a plain number is expected — position/scale/rotation array
elements, "offset", any "params" field (e.g. params.radius) — using the
string form:

  "ref:<objectId>.<bbox_min|bbox_max|center>.<x|y|z>"

  { "id": "sword", "position": ["ref:sheath.bbox_max.x", 0, 0] }
  { "id": "cap", "params": { "radius": "ref:jar.bbox_max.x" } }

Resolved in a deferred pass (same pattern PART 31/32 already used for
pendingLookAtTargets/pendingCSGOps) so forward references — an object
that appears earlier in the "objects" array than the thing it points
at — still work; the renderer retries once every mesh in the array
exists. A "ref:" string with a bad object id or bad field name never
crashes the build — it logs a console.warn and falls back to 0.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A4. Screen-space toon outline — [FIXED] second "outlineMode"
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

v7.1's toon outline (PART 32 #18) only had one technique — a scaled/
flipped back-face clone — which looks bad on thin or single-sided
geometry (feathers, cloth planes, leaves): the clone can pierce or
disappear on open, non-solid meshes. v7.2 adds a second mode: a
screen-space depth+normal edge-detect ShaderPass in the existing
EffectComposer pipeline, which draws an outline wherever neighboring
pixels' depth or normal buffers jump sharply, regardless of whether the
underlying geometry is closed. Opt in at the blueprint root:

  { "outlineMode": "screenspace", ... }

Omitting "outlineMode" (or setting it to "backface") keeps the original
v7.1 behavior — nothing about existing blueprints changes unless this
field is added.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A5. Triangle-budget LOD — [FIXED] alternate "lod" authoring mode
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

v7.1's LOD (PART 30/32 #5) required hand-picking a reduction fraction
("detail": 0.4, etc.) per distance level — awkward when you actually
care about a hard triangle ceiling and don't know the object's raw
triangle count offhand. v7.2 adds an alternate per-level field:

  { "lod": { "levels": [
      { "distance": 12, "targetTriangles": 800 },
      { "distance": 30, "targetTriangles": 250 }
  ]}}

The renderer measures the object's actual triangle count at build time
and computes the equivalent SimplifyModifier reduction fraction itself
(clamped 0.02-0.95) — same underlying decimation machinery as A1/the
original "decimate" operation, just budget-driven instead of fraction-
driven. A level can mix "detail" and "targetTriangles" across different
levels of the same object's "levels" array; "targetTriangles" wins for
whichever level sets it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A6. Joint weight blending — [FIXED] buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport()
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

v7.1's one-bone-per-part skinning (PART 32 #9) bound every vertex 100% to
its own part's bone — correct-looking at bind pose, but posed/exported
rigs crease sharply at every joint in Blender/a GLB viewer, since nothing
smooths the transition where one part's bone rotates relative to its
parent's. v7.2 blends skin weight between a part's own bone and its
parent's bone for vertices in roughly the last 15% of the part's length
nearest the joint boundary (the same boundary point A2 computes), with a
simple linear falloff — vertices right at the joint end up close to
50/50, vertices far from it stay 100% their own bone, same as before.
Deliberately simple (a single straight-line falloff along one axis, not
true geodesic distance) — non-degenerate, not aiming for production-
grade smooth skinning. Nothing to author; this applies automatically to
every exported skinned rig, including ones built from an explicit
blueprint.skeleton map, not just A2's fallback path.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART B — NEW ADDITIONS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

B1. Subdivision surface smoothing — [NEW] "subdivisionLevel": 1-3

  { "id": "egg", "generator": "superellipsoid", "params": {...},
    "subdivisionLevel": 2 }

Loop-style triangle subdivision (splits each triangle into 4 AND
repositions vertices via the standard Loop interior/boundary averaging
rule) — the low-poly triangle-mesh relative of Catmull-Clark quad
subdivision, not literally Catmull-Clark itself, since every generator
here produces triangles. Applied AFTER taper/twist/bend/CSG and BEFORE
the smoothing-groups pass, so it composes with everything already in the
pipeline. Each level roughly quadruples triangle count — levels above 2
get expensive fast on anything but small parts. Opt-in per object; omit
the field and geometry is untouched, exactly as before v7.2.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B2. Curve/spline sweep generator — [NEW] "generator": "sweep"
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

  { "id": "vine", "generator": "sweep", "params": {
      "profile": [[0.06,0],[0.04,0.04],[-0.04,0.04],[-0.06,0]],
      "path": [[0,0,0],[0.3,0.6,0.1],[0.5,1.4,-0.2],[0.2,2.0,0.3]]
  }}

Extrudes a 2D cross-section profile (same [[x,y],...] format genLathe/
genExtrudedPolygon already used) along a THREE.CatmullRomCurve3 built
from the "path" waypoints, using the curve's Frenet frames to orient the
cross-section at each step. For cables, vines, pipes, roads, and rivers
that need to bend smoothly instead of being chained together from
straight primitive segments. "profile" needs at least 3 points; "path"
needs at least 2 waypoints (2 waypoints = a straight sweep, still valid,
just not curved).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B3. Standalone vertex weld — [NEW] "type": "weld" operation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

  { "operations": [ { "type": "weld", "params": { "tolerance": 0.0005 } } ] }

Runs mergeVertices independent of any boolean op — useful any time two
separately-authored parts get merged and need a seamless connection
without a CSG operation being involved at all (e.g. after a "merge"
operation, or on a hand-modeled mesh with duplicate seam vertices from a
generator). "tolerance" is optional, defaults to 0.0001. This is the same
underlying weld A1 already runs automatically after a boolean op — B3 is
just that same tool exposed directly as its own operation.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B4. Cavity shading (fake curvature AO) — [NEW] "cavityShading": true
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

  { "id": "rockface", ..., "cavityShading": true }
  { "id": "statue",   ..., "cavityShading": 0.5 }

Darkens vertices that sit in a concave crease — where the local
neighborhood of connected vertices bulges OUTWARD past a vertex along its
own averaged normal — leaving convex ridges untouched (those already
catch direct light and don't need help reading as "finished"). Baked
into vertex colors, multiplied in on top of whatever color the vertex
already has (works whether or not "colorVariation" is also set — they
combine). Accepts either "true" (default strength 0.35) or a 0-1 number
for a custom strength. This is the single cheapest, highest-value visual
upgrade in this whole pass — it's what separates "hand-modeled-looking"
low-poly art from flat/generated-looking geometry.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B5. Triplanar UV fallback — [NEW] automatic on CSG'd/heavily-sculpted geometry
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

No JSON field — this is automatic. ensureUVs() previously applied a
single box/planar projection normalized to 0-1 over the object's full
bounding box, which visibly stretches "texturePattern" (wood/stone/
metal_brushed/etc) on non-cubic results — elongated shapes especially.
v7.2 flags any geometry that comes out of a boolean CSG op, the B2 sweep
generator, or a twist/bend sculpt beyond a 25° threshold, and gives those
specifically a triplanar-style projection driven by real world-unit
distances (not normalized to the bounding box), which removes the
aspect-ratio stretch. Everything else keeps the original v7.1 projection
unchanged.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B6. Character/liquid material presets — [NEW] "material": "skin"|"eye"|"glass"|"liquid"
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

MAT_DB previously skewed entirely toward hard-surface/metal/rock/wood —
nothing for characters or liquids. Four presets added:

  "skin"   — warm sheen-based rolloff (low roughness + sheen/sheenColor)
             approximating a soft subsurface-ish look without an actual
             subsurface-scattering shader.
  "eye"    — high clearcoat + low clearcoat-roughness, for a wet, glossy
             sclera/cornea look.
  "glass"  — REAL transmission via MeshPhysicalMaterial's transmission/
             ior/thickness properties (this build's THREE version
             confirmed to support them), not the old flat-alpha fake
             transparency other materials still use.
  "liquid" — transparent with high roughness VARIANCE (a per-object
             randomized roughness jitter, seeded by B8's global seed when
             set) so it reads as a disturbed liquid surface instead of
             polished glass.

Any object can also set "transmission"/"roughnessVariance" directly
without using one of these preset names, for a custom in-between look.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B7. Scene-wide instance deduplication — [NEW] automatic in finalizeSceneBuild()
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

No JSON field — automatic. Previously only array/scatter OPERATIONS
(lineararray/gridarray/radialarray/scatter/distributeEven) got
InstancedMesh treatment (unchanged since v6.6). Two independently
hand-placed objects elsewhere in "objects" with identical geometry+
material never merged. v7.2 adds a post-build pass that groups top-level
meshes by a cheap fingerprint (vertex count + bounding-box dimensions +
material key — not a full geometry hash, so two objects with the same
vertex count and bbox but meaningfully different shape could in theory
collide; acceptable given the low-poly primitives this renderer
generates) and converts any group of INSTANCING_THRESHOLD (6) or more
into a single InstancedMesh — same threshold the existing array-operation
system already uses.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
B8. Blueprint-level global seed — [NEW] "seed": 12345 (top level)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

  { "seed": 12345, "objects": [ ... ] }

Threads one deterministic PRNG (a small mulberry32-style generator, not
cryptographic — doesn't need to be) into every noise/jitter-consuming
function that didn't already take its own explicit seed: genVoronoi's
jitter, the "noise" operation, and applyColorVariation's per-face color
jitter. "scatter" already took its own per-operation seed (default 42,
unchanged) — B8 only changes what it falls back to when an object omits
"seed" entirely, so it's still reproducible without the author having to
set one manually. genFractal already took an explicit "seed" param before
v7.2 and is unaffected. Omitting the top-level "seed" field keeps the
old behavior exactly — every rebuild redraws differently, same as always
before this existed. Set it once an author is happy with a model, and
reloading/rebuilding the same JSON reproduces it exactly.

================================================================================
END OF v7.2 IMAGE-TO-JS SPECIFICATION
This fork adds PART 27 (IMAGE-TO-JS ANALYSIS PROTOCOL) near the top, and
PART 32 (JS OUTPUT FORMAT), PART 33 (v7.1 ADDITIONS), and PART 34 (v7.2
ADDITIONS) at the end.
If you started reading from the bottom: go back to PART 27 before generating.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 34-R — v7.3 RENDERER ADDITIONS (verified against low_poly_3d.html, 2026-07-04)
================================================================================
[CONTRADICTION FIX, v7.8/PART 54] This section was mis-numbered "PART 34"
when first written, colliding with the PRECEDING section (also "PART 34" —
v7.2 ADDITIONS, the one with A1-A6/B1-B8 lettered items just above this).
Renumbering everything from here to the end of the document (PART 35
onward) to close the gap would touch dozens of cross-references throughout
this file for a cosmetic fix, so instead this section is relabeled "34-R"
to make the two unambiguous at a glance — its own internal sub-numbering
(34.1-34.7 below) is UNCHANGED, so every existing cross-reference like
"PART 34.2" or "PART 34.7" elsewhere in this document still points at
exactly the same place it always did. See PART 23's changelog for when
this was caught and fixed.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Six additions closing gaps identified against a competing renderer's output
quality. All are additive — nothing existing changed behavior.

34.1 — ANCHOR NAME ALIASES
  The existing named string anchors (PART 6/7: "top_center", "bottom_center",
  "left_center", "right_center", "front_center", "back_center", plus
  "bbox_min"/"bbox_max"/"center") now also accept "local"/"world"-prefixed
  spellings for the same six face points, in any casing:
    "localTop" / "local_top" / "worldTop" / "worldtop"   → same as "top_center"
    "localBottom" / "worldBottom"                         → same as "bottom_center"
    "localLeft" / "worldLeft"                             → same as "left_center"
    "localRight" / "worldRight"                           → same as "right_center"
    "localFront" / "worldFront"                           → same as "front_center"
    "localBack" / "worldBack"                              → same as "back_center"
  All anchor strings are now case/underscore-insensitive generally (matches
  how "type" has always been normalized) — "TopCenter", "top_center", and
  "topcenter" all resolve identically.

34.2 — "sideOf" / "sideToward" PLACEMENT
  A new placement mode alongside attachTo/placement/anchor. Use it as a
  top-level "on" object (or inside an "anchor" object):
    { "id": "mount", "type": "frustum_box", "on": { "sideOf": "band", "sideToward": "worldTop" }, ... }
  "sideToward" accepts worldTop/worldBottom/worldLeft/worldRight/worldFront/
  worldBack (aliases: top/bottom/left/right/front/back). Unlike "placement"
  (measured off the parent's world-space AABB, which silently picks the
  wrong face once the parent is rotated), this resolves the face in the
  PARENT'S OWN LOCAL SPACE first and only then projects it into world space
  — so it stays correct even when the parent is tilted/rotated. "sideOf"
  also works as the object's attachment target on its own (you don't need a
  separate "attachTo" pointing at the same id).

34.3 — "frustum_box" / "frustumbox" PRIMITIVE
  Independent base/tip rectangle taper, for when "truncated_pyramid"'s fixed
  uniform 0.5:0.25 square ratio isn't enough (asymmetric tapers, or a tip
  LARGER than the base — a flare, not just a shrink):
    { "id": "diamondBase", "type": "frustum_box",
      "footprintSize": [1.5, 1.5],   // base rectangle [width, depth]
      "tipSize":       [14, 14],     // tip rectangle [width, depth] — can exceed footprintSize
      "length": 6,                   // height (defaults to scale.y)
      "material": "diamond" }
  footprintSize/tipSize each default to the object's own scale if omitted;
  tipSize defaults to footprintSize (a straight prism) if omitted.

34.4 — "chain" GENERATOR ("type":"generated","generator":"chain")
  A friendlier front end to the existing "tube" generator (PART 25), for
  continuous tapered organic forms (tentacles, necks, horns, mantles).
  Instead of hand-computing an absolute local "path" + parallel "radii"
  array (tube's #1 real-world mistake: writing world coordinates into path
  by accident, which detaches the tube from the model), author a sequence
  of segments as direction + length + taper:
    { "id": "tentacle0", "type": "generated", "generator": "chain",
      "params": {
        "baseRadius": 0.3,
        "segs": 8,
        "segments": [
          { "length": 1.5, "tipRatio": 0.8, "aim": [0.5, -1, 0.2] },
          { "length": 2.0, "tipRatio": 0.6, "aim": [1.0, -0.6, 0.8] },
          { "length": 1.5, "tipRatio": 0.3, "aim": [1.5, -0.1, 1.5] }
        ]
      },
      "position": [0.6, 0.4, 0], "material": "ghost" }
  Rules:
    - "aim" is a DIRECTION only — its magnitude is ignored/normalized
      internally, so [1,0,0] and [5,0,0] behave identically. "length"
      alone controls how far the segment travels.
    - Each segment automatically starts where the previous one ended.
    - "tipRatio" is relative to that segment's OWN base radius (i.e. the
      previous segment's tip) — 0.6 always means "60% of whatever radius
      this segment started at", not a fraction of baseRadius.
    - If "segments" is omitted, "chain" behaves exactly like "tube"
      (reads path/radii directly) — fully backward compatible.

34.5 — "hollow" OPERATION
  New entry in the "operations" array (PART 9) for shelling a solid
  cylindrical/band shape into a real hole-through shape (ring bands, pipes,
  collars) without a separate CSG boolean pass:
    { "id": "band", "type": "cylinder", "scale": [1.2, 0.4, 1.2],
      "material": "platinum",
      "operations": [ { "type": "hollow",
        "params": { "wallThickness": 0.12, "openFaces": ["base","tip"] } } ] }
  params: wallThickness (world units, default 15% of the object's own
  radius), openFaces (array; "base"/"bottom" and/or "tip"/"top" — faces
  left OPEN with no cap; default both open, i.e. a plain finger-hole ring),
  axis ("x"|"y"|"z", default "y"), segs (default 24). Rebuilds the mesh's
  geometry directly from its own bounding box, so it works on whatever
  primitive/generator produced it.

34.6 — "mirrorFace" CONSTRAINT
  Generalizes the existing "symmetrical" constraint (PART 8, which only
  mirrors X) to either or both of an object's two horizontal axes at once,
  as a top-level field (not inside "constraints"):
    { "id": "prong", "type": "frustum_box", "on": { "anchor": "worldTop" },
      "footprintSize": [1.2,1.2], "tipSize": [0.6,0.6], "length": 7,
      "material": "platinum", "mirrorFace": ["u","v"] }
  "mirrorFace": ["u"] mirrors once across X; ["v"] mirrors once across Z;
  ["u","v"] produces all 4 copies (original + 3 mirrors) from one authored
  object — e.g. one ring prong becomes all four corner prongs. Mirroring
  is around the attachTo parent's bounding-box center (or this object's own
  position if it has no parent). Aliases "x"/"z" are accepted for "u"/"v".
  NOTE — current limitation: like "symmetrical", this mirrors the object
  ITSELF only. If other objects are attachTo'd to a mirrorFace object
  expecting to inherit the mirror too, they won't automatically follow —
  author those separately for now. Full subtree mirroring (mirror a parent
  AND everything attached to it in one step) is a known gap, not yet
  implemented.

34.7 — "asymmetry" IS NOW LIVE (previously documentation-only — corrected 2026-07-05)
  IMPORTANT CHANGE: PART 26.8's "asymmetry" block was previously an
  authoring CONVENTION ONLY — the renderer never read it, so the actual
  tilt/drop/shift had to be hand-duplicated into skeleton coordinates and
  per-object "rotation" fields. That duplication is exactly what produced
  a real bug in this spec's own "Heavy Combat Robot" reference example
  (PART 26.11): it declared "shoulder_l_drop":0.03 while the skeleton
  coordinates actually had shoulder_r sitting lower — the label and the
  geometry disagreed. As of v7.3, blueprint.asymmetry is read automatically
  by the renderer, BEFORE any object is placed:
    "asymmetry": {
      "head_tilt": 3,          // degrees, added to Z-rotation of any
                                 // object whose "joint" is "head"
                                 // (additive with that object's own
                                 // explicit "rotation", not a replacement)
      "shoulder_l_drop": 0.03,  // moves skeleton.shoulder_l DOWN 0.03 units
      "hips_shift": -0.02       // moves skeleton.hips BACKWARD 0.02 units
    }
  Supported key suffixes (key prefix must match a skeleton joint name):
    <joint>_drop   float   skeleton joint moves DOWN (-Y) by this amount
    <joint>_lift   float   skeleton joint moves UP (+Y) by this amount
    <joint>_shift  float   skeleton joint moves along +Z by this amount
                            (negative = backward)
    <joint>_tilt   degrees additive Z-rotation on any object riding that joint
  AUTHORING RULE: pick ONE place to express a given asymmetry — either
  hand-bake it into "skeleton" coordinates and per-object "rotation", OR
  declare it in "asymmetry" and leave the skeleton symmetric — never both,
  or the two will double up (tilt) or silently contradict each other
  (drop/shift), same as the bug above. "asymmetry" is the preferred way
  going forward since it can't fall out of sync the way manual duplication
  did.

================================================================================
PART 35 — v7.4 ADDITIONS: offsetRatio, DYNAMIC ANCHOR BASE POINT, BUILDER API
================================================================================
[CONTRADICTION FIX, v7.8/PART 54] This section's original 35.1 re-described
"asymmetry" becoming a live renderer feature as though it were a NEW fix in
v7.4 — it wasn't. PART 34-R.7 (v7.3) already made this exact change one
version earlier, using the same "Heavy Combat Robot" audit example and the
same before/after description; the two write-ups were never reconciled and
duplicated each other. 35.1 below is now just a pointer to the canonical
description at PART 34-R.7 — nothing about the mechanism itself changed.
35.2-35.4 are genuinely new to v7.4 and unaffected by this fix.

35.1 — "asymmetry" is live — see PART 34-R.7 (not a separate v7.4 change)
  See PART 34-R.7 for the live-apply mechanism (the four "<joint>_drop" /
  "_lift" / "_shift" / "_tilt" keys, applied to blueprint.skeleton before
  any object is placed) and the corrected "Heavy Combat Robot" example.
  Reminder: "proportions" (PART 6 upgrade 2) is still INFO-ONLY — it drives
  the inspector panel display, not geometry. Skeleton coordinates and each
  object's own params are what actually shape the model; keep proportions
  numbers consistent with them by hand, the renderer doesn't cross-check.

35.2 — "offsetRatio" ON DYNAMIC ANCHOR OBJECTS
  Alongside the existing "offset" (absolute world units), a dynamic anchor
  object now also accepts "offsetRatio": [uRatio, vRatio], scaled by the
  PARENT's own half-extents on X/Z:
    "anchor": { "anchor": "worldTop", "offsetRatio": [0.85, 0.85] }
  This places the child 85% of the way to the parent's corner regardless of
  the parent's actual size — useful for corner-mounted details (rivets,
  prongs, corner trim) that should track a parent's dimensions if it's
  resized later, instead of needing hand-recomputed absolute offsets.
  "offset" and "offsetRatio" can combine (both are added to the base point).

35.3 — DYNAMIC ANCHOR BASE POINT (nested "anchor" inside "anchor")
  A dynamic anchor object can now start from a named face point on its
  target instead of always the parent's dead center:
    "anchor": { "anchor": "localLeft", "offset": [0, 0.2, 0] }
  starts from the parent's left face, then applies offset/offsetRatio from
  there — instead of only ever being able to offset from the exact center.

35.4 — BUILDER-STYLE AUTHORING API (JS blueprint files only)
  A real, callable alternative to writing an object literal — matching the
  attachBox/attachCylinder/attachChain/group/createAnchor/definePalette/
  hollow/paintRectangle authoring style directly:
    name("Ghostopus");
    definePalette({ ghost: { color:'#e0ffff', roughness:0.1, opacity:0.8 } });
    createAnchor('bodyOrigin');
    attachChain('mantle', {
      on: 'bodyOrigin.localTop',
      segments: [
        { length:1.5, tipRatio:1.15, aim:[0,1,0] },
        { length:2.5, tipRatio:0.85, aim:[0,1,-0.1] }
      ],
      material: 'ghost'
    });
    group('fins', { mirrorWorld:'x' }, () => {
      attachBox('fin', { on:'mantle_seg1.localLeft', length:2.5, tipRatio:0.2, material:'ghost' });
    });
  These calls are a thin front-end over fields the renderer already has
  (frustum_box, the chain generator, the hollow operation, sideOf/
  sideToward, mirrorFace, offsetRatio) — NOT a new execution engine. A
  script using them doesn't need an explicit "return {...}"; if nothing
  is returned, the buffer these calls filled is assembled into
  {meta, objects} automatically. You can still write plain object-literal/
  return-style JS blueprints exactly as before — both styles work in the
  same file type and can even mix within one script.
  Known limitation (stated plainly, not glossed over): named face anchors
  (top/bottom/left/right/front/back and their local*/world* aliases) are
  resolved off each object's WORLD-SPACE AXIS-ALIGNED bounding box — they
  do not rotate with an object's own tilt. A rotated attachChain segment's
  "localLeft" is still a world-axis-aligned point, not one rotated into
  that segment's own tilted frame. This is the same limitation already
  noted for sideOf/sideToward (PART 34.2) — true oriented-bounding-box
  face resolution is a larger change, not yet done.

================================================================================
PART 36 — REFERENTIAL INTEGRITY: MANDATORY FINAL SELF-CHECK PASS
================================================================================
PARTs 36-45 are additive — everything in PARTs 1-35 is unchanged and still
accurate. They consolidate a model-integrity, pose, and limb-connectivity
fix (LBL_Model_Integrity_And_Pose_Fix_v1.txt) that traces back to two root
causes, confirmed directly against low_poly_3d.html's source rather than
inferred:

  (1) Every object's position is a ONE-TIME world-space snapshot computed
      once when it's first built (solvePosition()). There is no true
      parent-child Three.js hierarchy for anything except GLB export —
      rotating/reposing a parent never moves its children, and a "joint"
      coordinate authored for one pose never updates when something
      upstream of it changes. See PART 40 for the full character-posing
      consequences.
  (2) Every "connect this to that by name" field (attachTo, parent, joint,
      sideOf, CSG "with", lookAt, "ref:" strings) trusted the string it
      was given completely. If the id didn't resolve, each mechanism
      failed in its own silent, undetected way — no error, no warning,
      just a part that quietly renders in the wrong place or not at all.

Together these explain nearly every "why is this part floating / detached
/ broken" result that ships with a clean validation log: a hand's position
computed once and never revisited (1), referencing a joint id that may
itself have drifted or been slightly mistyped (2) — so it silently ended
up somewhere wrong with no error anywhere in the pipeline.

The renderer's validateBlueprintSchema() now catches every case of (2)
directly — a dangling id reference is pushed as a hard error, the same way
"material must be a string" already is. But the AI generating a blueprint
never sees that validation log; only the person does, after pasting the
result into the renderer (PART 39). So this is also an authoring
discipline, not just a renderer safety net: before returning any
blueprint, do a final pass that lists every id you actually created, then
walks every attachTo/parent/joint/sideOf/lookAt/operations[].with/"ref:"
string in the blueprint and confirms each one is in that list,
character-for-character. Watch specifically for:
  - case mismatches, e.g. "Hand_L" vs "hand_l"
  - underscore drift, e.g. "arm_l" vs "arml"
  - referencing an object that was ALSO consumed as a CSG "with" target
    elsewhere — its id stops existing in the live scene the moment that
    boolean runs, so anything else trying to attachTo it afterward fails
    the same silent way.

This matters more than it sounds: unlike a bad material or an unknown
generator (which degrade gracefully to a visible fallback), every one of
these reference fields fails with NO visible sign anything is wrong — the
model just looks subtly (or badly) broken with a clean validation log.
This is the single highest-value check to actually run before calling a
model finished.

================================================================================
PART 37 — placement VS sideOf/sideToward ON A ROTATED PARENT
================================================================================
"placement" (below/above/front/left/etc., PART 7) resolves off the
parent's WORLD-SPACE axis-aligned bounding box. That's fine for an
unrotated parent, but the moment a parent has any non-zero "rotation"
field, that AABB stops lining up with the parent's actual oriented shape
— "placement" will silently pick a point that's on the wrong face, or not
actually touching the parent's visible surface, with zero warning.
sideOf/sideToward (PART 34.2) exist specifically to solve this: they
resolve in the parent's LOCAL space before projecting into world space, so
they find the true face regardless of rotation.

RULE: any object attaching to a parent that has a non-zero "rotation"
field (or that sits at the end of a bent chain/tube) must use
sideOf/sideToward, never plain "placement". This applies to a rotated
turret, a tilted signpost, an angled wing panel, a leaning tree trunk —
anything with rotation, not only limbs.

================================================================================
PART 38 — CSG TIMING: ATTACHMENTS RESOLVE AGAINST THE PRE-BOOLEAN SHAPE
================================================================================
Boolean operations (PART 10) run in a deferred pass AFTER every object's
normal position is already resolved. Anything that uses attachTo/placement
targeting a CSG base or tool id gets positioned against that object's
bounding box as it was BEFORE the cut/merge — not the final result.

  - Usually harmless for subtract/difference (an interior notch rarely
    changes the overall bounding box).
  - Real for union/add/merge (the resulting shape is often genuinely
    bigger than either input) — anything attached "on top of" or "beside"
    a unioned object was placed using the smaller pre-union footprint, and
    will visibly float or clip into the merged result.

RULE: never attachTo a CSG base/tool id for anything that needs to sit
against the object's FINAL silhouette. Give the union/merge result its own
separate downstream id-based attachment only after confirming (by eye, in
the renderer) where the merged shape's actual bounding box ended up, or
restructure so the thing being attached is unioned in as part of the same
boolean pass rather than attached afterward.

================================================================================
PART 39 — WORKFLOW: ALWAYS READ THE VALIDATION LOG
================================================================================
The AI generating a blueprint never sees validateBlueprintSchema()'s own
warnings/errors — only the person does, after pasting the result into the
renderer. None of PART 36's checks help if that validation log never gets
read. Treat "paste it into the renderer and read the validation log top to
bottom, including warnings" as a mandatory last step before considering
any generated model finished — not just for errors (which block the build
and are hard to miss) but for warnings (which don't block anything and are
easy to skip past, even though several of them — unknown
generator/material/type falling back to a default shape — are exactly the
kind of thing that produces a "why does this one part look wrong" result).

================================================================================
PART 40 — HUMANOID/CREATURE LIMBS: BUILD EACH LIMB AS ONE CONTINUOUS MESH
================================================================================
DIAGNOSIS — why limbs/hands/props keep detaching
Verified directly against low_poly_3d.html's buildScene(): every object is
added with `sceneGroup.add(mesh)` — a FLAT list, not a real parent-child
Three.js hierarchy (root cause 1, PART 36). Rotating "upper_arm_l" does NOT
move "forearm_l" or "hand_l" — nothing in the live viewer propagates a
parent's transform to its children. (Real bone parenting only exists in
buildSkinnedHierarchyForExport(), used for GLB export — irrelevant to what
renders on screen.) Consequence: for ANY multi-part limb, every downstream
part's final position must be independently correct and mathematically
consistent with its neighbor, or it will visibly drift apart. There is no
"pose the rig, everything follows" behavior — ever.

This is what produces the most common limb/prop/garment bugs:
  - Hands floating away from sleeves: a hand's position came from a
    blueprint.skeleton coordinate (or hand-typed "position") authored for
    ONE arm layout, while the arm segment above it was rotated/repositioned
    afterward without recomputing the hand to match.
  - Full T-pose: "subtle asymmetry, not a stiff mirrored T-pose" (PART
    26.8) is satisfiable with small tilts while arms stay fully
    horizontal — it never explicitly forbids a horizontal T-pose outright
    on its own (see PART 42).
  - Floating held props: a "held" object (staff, cup, tool) was given its
    own absolute "position" instead of attachTo-ing the hand, so it
    doesn't track the hand at all (see PART 43).
  - Flat floating garment overlays (apron): front-only coverage is
    realistic, but with no strap/tie geometry connecting it to the body
    and no "overlap", it reads as a separate hovering plane instead of a
    worn garment — especially obvious from the back (see PART 44).

A second, contributing cause: PART 25's "capsule" generator worked example
used to show a "forearm_l" object with a hand-typed absolute position AND
rotation, with no attachTo/chain connection to anything — i.e. it directly
demonstrated the broken pattern, while PART 28.6 elsewhere recommends
"tube"/"chain" for curved limbs instead. A concrete worked example tends to
win over an abstract rule sitting a thousand lines away, so PART 25's
example has been corrected to match this PART instead.

RULE — never author "upper_arm_l" and "forearm_l" (or "upper_leg"/
"lower_leg") as two separate objects placed with independently hand-typed
position/rotation numbers. Use the "chain" generator (a friendlier front
end to "tube", already in the renderer — PART 34.4) to build the ENTIRE
limb — shoulder to wrist, or hip to ankle — as a single mesh. Each segment
starts exactly where the previous one ends, by construction, so a gap
between "upper arm" and "forearm" is structurally impossible:

  {
    "id": "arm_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "chain",
    "params": {
      "baseRadius": 0.075, "segs": 6,
      "segments": [
        { "length": 0.40, "tipRatio": 0.85, "aim": [0.08, -1, 0.05] },
        { "length": 0.35, "tipRatio": 0.65, "aim": [0.10, -1, 0.28] }
      ]
    },
    "position": [0.42, 1.35, 0],
    "material": "fur", "baseColor": "#5f8f6f"
  }

"aim" is a DIRECTION only (magnitude ignored) — author it pointing where
the limb should actually travel (mostly -Y for a hanging arm), not a raw
rotation you have to guess in degrees.

If a limb needs a material/color change partway down (e.g. short sleeve
then bare forearm then glove), split it into AT MOST as many objects as
there are material changes — never split simply because it's "anatomically
two bones." Connect each material change with PART 41 below (attachTo +
placement), never with a second hand-typed skeleton coordinate.

QUICK REFERENCE — anti-pattern vs fix:

  ANTI-PATTERN (produces a floating hand with no error anywhere):
    "skeleton": {
      "shoulder_l": [0.42, 1.35, 0],
      "elbow_l":    [0.80, 1.35, 0],
      "hand_l":     [1.15, 1.35, 0]
    },
    "objects": [
      { "id": "upper_arm_l", "type":"generated", "generator":"capsule",
        "params": {"radius":0.08,"length":0.35},
        "joint": "shoulder_l", "rotation": [0,0,90] },
      { "id": "hand_l", "type":"generated", "generator":"capsule",
        "params": {"radius":0.09,"length":0.05},
        "joint": "hand_l" }
    ]
  Once "upper_arm_l" gets a manual +90° rotation to hang down, "hand_l"'s
  skeleton coordinate — still the original T-pose value — is never
  recomputed to match, so it floats at the old location.

  FIXED (this PART + PART 41):
    "objects": [
      { "id": "arm_l", "type":"generated", "generator":"chain",
        "params": { "baseRadius":0.075, "segs":6, "segments":[
          { "length":0.40, "tipRatio":0.85, "aim":[0.08,-1,0.05] },
          { "length":0.35, "tipRatio":0.65, "aim":[0.10,-1,0.28] }
        ]},
        "position": [0.42, 1.35, 0],
        "material":"fur", "baseColor":"#5f8f6f" },
      { "id": "hand_l", "type":"generated", "generator":"capsule",
        "params": {"radius":0.09,"length":0.02},
        "attachTo": "arm_l", "placement": "below",
        "material":"skin", "baseColor":"#e8c39e" }
    ]
  This cannot produce a gap: the hand's position is derived from the arm
  mesh's actual built geometry every time, regardless of how the arm's
  segments are angled.

================================================================================
PART 41 — TERMINAL ATTACHMENTS (hands/feet/cuffs) VIA attachTo + placement
================================================================================
Hands, feet, sleeve cuffs, and any other part riding the END of a limb must
use attachTo (targeting the limb object's own id) + placement — NEVER a
hand-typed "position". placement is computed off the limb's REAL BUILT
bounding box, so it self-corrects no matter how the chain's segments were
angled:

  {
    "id": "hand_l", "type": "generated", "generator": "capsule",
    "params": { "radius": 0.09, "length": 0.02 },
    "attachTo": "arm_l", "placement": "below",
    "material": "skin", "baseColor": "#e8c39e"
  }

Pick the placement direction that matches the limb's dominant lean:
"below" for a mostly-vertical hanging arm/leg, "front" for a
forward-reaching arm, etc. (If the limb has rotation, use sideOf/sideToward
instead — see PART 37.)

>>> IMPORTANT — do NOT also give this object a "joint" field naming a real
key in blueprint.skeleton, even "just for documentation." Verified in
buildScene(): the skeleton joint-placement step runs BEFORE isRoot/
solvePosition and unconditionally sets `obj.position =
blueprint.skeleton[obj.joint]` the instant "joint" resolves — which then
behaves exactly like a hand-typed "position" (PART 4) and completely
overrides attachTo/placement, with no warning from the build itself. A
resolving "joint" always wins over attachTo, silently, regardless of
intent — the schema validator (PART 36) now flags this specific
combination too, but the safer habit is to just never author it: if you
want the original skeleton coordinate kept around for reference, note it
in a comment near the "skeleton" block, not as a live "joint" field on a
part whose final position attachTo+placement is supposed to control.

================================================================================
PART 42 — DEFAULT POSE IS RELAXED, NOT A T-POSE
================================================================================
Unless the brief explicitly asks for "T-pose" / "reference pose" / "bind
pose", a standing humanoid/creature's arms hang roughly VERTICALLY at the
sides (aim mostly -Y), with a mild forward/inward bend at the elbow (a
touch more +Z or inward X on the forearm segment's aim vs. the upper arm's
— see PART 40's "chain" segments for how to author this). A fully
horizontal arms-out T-pose is banned as a default — "subtle asymmetry"
(PART 26.8) is a separate, additional touch on top of a relaxed base pose,
not a substitute for one.

================================================================================
PART 43 — HELD PROPS ATTACH TO THE HAND, NEVER FLOAT ON THEIR OWN
================================================================================
Anything "held" (staff, cup, tool, weapon) must use attachTo:"hand_l" (or
"hand_r") + placement/anchor, with a small rotation for a natural grip
angle, and its own bounding box must actually overlap the hand's:

  {
    "id": "staff", "type": "cylinder", "scale": [0.02, 0.9, 0.02],
    "attachTo": "hand_l", "placement": "below", "offset": [0, 0.35, 0],
    "rotation": [4, 0, 2],
    "material": "wood", "baseColor": "#3a2a1f"
  }

Every decorative/small object in the scene must have a clear purpose tied
to the brief. Before output, re-scan the "objects" array for any stray
object with no attachTo/placement/joint that isn't a deliberate scene
root (ground clutter, etc.) — if you can't say in one sentence what it's
for, delete it.

================================================================================
PART 44 — GARMENT OVERLAYS NEED STRAPS AND overlap, NOT JUST A FLAT PANEL
================================================================================
An apron/vest/harness that only covers the front is realistic — but it
needs thin strap/tie objects (neck loop, waist ties) attachTo-ed to the
torso to visually anchor it from the side and back views, plus
"overlap": 0.04–0.08 (PART 26.5) against the torso so it doesn't read as a
plane hovering in front of the body. Check clothing overlays from BOTH
front AND back (extend PART 26.9's silhouette check) — a front-only flat
panel with no back-view geometry reads as broken, not as "an apron."

================================================================================
PART 45 — MANUAL FK HELPER (fallback ONLY — prefer PART 40's "chain" first)
================================================================================
Use this only when a single "chain" mesh genuinely doesn't fit (e.g. you
need per-segment materials the chain generator can't express, or you want
individual capsule objects). This is real JavaScript — it runs at build
time in this .js pipeline, where you have no access to THREE.js classes
(PART 32.2: "no access to the page's other variables"), only plain Math:

  // Orients a Y-axis limbSegment (capsule/cylinder default long axis) to
  // point from `start` to `end`. Roll/twist around the segment's own axis
  // is visually irrelevant for a round cross-section, so only 2 of 3
  // Euler angles matter here — good enough for single-plane limb bends.
  // For a compound (diagonal) bend, prefer two straight segments (PART 40)
  // over one segment with a 3-axis twist — also more anatomically correct,
  // since real elbows/knees are single-plane hinges.
  function limbSegment(start, end){
    const dx = end[0]-start[0], dy = end[1]-start[1], dz = end[2]-start[2];
    const length = Math.hypot(dx, dy, dz);
    const mid = [ (start[0]+end[0])/2, (start[1]+end[1])/2, (start[2]+end[2])/2 ];
    const rotX = Math.atan2(dz, Math.hypot(dx, dy)) * -180 / Math.PI; // forward/back lean
    const rotZ = Math.atan2(dx, dy) * 180 / Math.PI;                  // side-to-side lean
    return { position: mid, rotation: [rotX, 0, rotZ], length };
  }

  // Usage — always derive the NEXT joint from THIS segment's actual end
  // point, never re-type it independently:
  const shoulder = [0.42, 1.35, 0];
  const elbow    = [0.50, 1.00, 0.06];               // chosen pose, not T-pose
  const upperArm = limbSegment(shoulder, elbow);
  const wrist    = [0.55, 0.68, 0.20];
  const forearm  = limbSegment(elbow, wrist);
  // hand_l then attaches via attachTo:"forearm_l" + placement (PART 41) —
  // NOT by re-typing `wrist` as its own position.


================================================================================
PART 46 — v7.5 ADDITIONS: AUTO-ENHANCEMENT GEOMETRY PIPELINE
================================================================================
This PART integrates a 13-technique classical-geometry-processing pass
(indexed face sets, Delaunay/greedy-projection terrain, ball pivoting,
marching cubes, Poisson reconstruction, RANSAC/least-squares plane fitting,
convex hull, region growing, Laplacian smoothing, normal estimation) that
was originally sketched as a standalone JS file, not written against this
renderer's actual code. Integrating it required checking each technique
against the real implementation rather than pasting it in directly — most
of it mapped cleanly onto features that already exist (PART 9's operations
engine, PART 12's sdf_blob, PART 26's smoothing/colorVariation), a few
techniques were redundant with existing, more accurate systems, and a few
had real bugs that would have made them silent no-ops. All of it is broken
down in 46.6. Verified against low_poly_3d.html on 2026-07-16.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.1 — OVERVIEW: "autoEnhance": true
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "autoEnhance": true, "objects": [ ... ] }

A single opt-in blueprint-level flag. Default is OFF/omitted, so no
existing blueprint's render changes unless this is added deliberately —
same backward-compatibility pattern as "autoScale" (PART 30.4) and
"outlineMode" (PART 33 A4). When on, the renderer fills in operations and
per-object fields you left unset — see 46.2-46.4 for exactly what. Every
rule it applies is gated on the target field being `undefined`, so
anything you DID set — explicitly, on any object — always wins and is
never touched. This makes the pass idempotent and safe to leave on while
iterating: re-pasting the same JSON, or live-editing a field from the
Inspector panel (which rebuilds via buildScene() directly, not just on
initial load), re-runs it and gets the same result.

This is a convenience layer for when you want a plausible, hand-crafted-
feeling default without authoring every operation/field by hand — it is
not a substitute for PART 20's semantic modeling guidance, and it will
never rescue a poorly-proportioned or under-built model.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.2 — TECHNIQUE MAP
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  1.  Indexed Face Set        [MAPPED]     auto-weld on "mesh" primitives
  2.  Triangle Soup           [MAPPED]     (same mechanism as #1)
  3.  Delaunay Triangulation  [MAPPED]     smooth terrain types: +subdivide+noise
  4.  Ball Pivoting (BPA)     [MAPPED, FIXED]  "bpa":true -> real sdf_blob
  5.  Greedy Projection       [MAPPED]     (same mechanism as #3)
  6.  Convex Hull             [NEW, REAL]  "addConvexHull" — see 46.5
  7.  Marching Cubes          [MAPPED]     sdf_blob "resolution" auto-scale
  8.  Poisson Reconstruction  [MAPPED, FIXED]  + real "subdivisionLevel" (B1)
  9.  RANSAC Plane Fit        [NOT PORTED] autoGround already does this for real
  10. Least-Squares Plane Fit [NOT PORTED] (same reasoning as #9)
  11. Region Growing          [MAPPED, IMPROVED]  colorVariation + cavityShading
  12. Laplacian Smoothing     [MAPPED, IMPROVED]  smoothing:"auto" (edge-aware)
  13. Normal Estimation       [MAPPED, FIXED]  smoothing field, corrected mat list

"IMPROVED" means the technique was retargeted onto a more capable
mechanism this renderer already has, rather than the cruder one the
original sketch used. "FIXED" means the original sketch's version of that
technique had a real, verified bug — see 46.6.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.3 — GEOMETRY-LEVEL TECHNIQUES (1, 2, 3, 5, 4, 7, 8)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Auto-weld (1, 2): every object with "type":"mesh" (raw author-supplied
vertices/faces — PART 24) gets a weld operation appended if it doesn't
already have one:
  { "type": "weld", "params": { "tolerance": 0.0005 } }
CSG boolean results already get an equivalent auto-weld unconditionally
(PART 33 A1) — this closes the one real remaining gap (hand-authored mesh
primitives) rather than duplicating A1.

Terrain refinement (3, 5): restricted to "ground_plane", "hill", "valley",
and "island". All four build a perfectly smooth, zero-randomness surface
in this renderer (verified directly against buildGeometryRaw()/
buildHill()/buildValley() — "island" literally calls buildHill() with a
scale tweak, so it's the same underlying shape). "mountain", "cliff", and
"rock_formation" already bake their own random irregularity into their
own generator functions — adding more on top would double-jitter an
already hand-tuned shape, so those three are deliberately left alone.
"plateau" is ALSO smooth/noise-free by this same test but is excluded
anyway: a flat top is that primitive's whole defining feature, not an
oversight, so roughening it fights the shape's own intent rather than
polishing it. There is no "terrain" primitive type in this renderer at
all (checked against every case label in buildGeometryRaw() — see 46.6
item 3). A qualifying object gets, if not already present:
  { "type": "subdivide", "params": { "iterations": 1 } }
  { "type": "noise",     "params": { "amount": 0.05 } }

Ball Pivoting (4): "bpa":true on ANY object converts it to a real
sdf_blob (the closest thing this renderer has to reconstructing a surface
from an unstructured point set):
  { "id": "rockClump", "type": "cube", "bpa": true }
becomes, at build time, "type":"sdf_blob" with a default 3-ball "balls"
array (PART 12) if you didn't supply one yourself. The object's original
"type" is preserved on "__bpaOriginalType" for reference (the renderer
itself never reads this back). If you already know what balls you want,
set "balls" explicitly alongside "bpa":true and your array is used as-is.

sdf_blob detail scaling (7, 8): an sdf_blob/metaball object with a
"detail" of 2 or more gets a higher marching-cubes "resolution" (36,
PART 12's normal default is 28) if you didn't set one; "detail" 3 or more
ALSO gets one level of real Loop subdivision via "subdivisionLevel" (PART
33 B1) if you didn't set one. Note "detail" isn't read anywhere else for
this primitive (buildSDFBlob only reads "resolution"/"balls") — it exists
here purely as this pass's own polish-level hint for sdf_blob objects,
the same way "detail" already scales segment counts on base primitives
elsewhere in PART 2.
  { "type": "sdf_blob", "detail": 3, "balls": [ ... ] }
  // -> resolution:36, subdivisionLevel:1 filled in automatically

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.4 — MATERIAL-LEVEL TECHNIQUES (11, 12, 13)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Two material lists drive these, checked against the real MAT_DB table:

  ORGANIC:  fur, skin, leaf, flower, grass, moss, vine, bark, cloth,
            cotton, linen, silk, wool, felt, canvas, leather
  POLISHED: glass, frostedglass, stainedglass, water, ice, chrome, eye,
            crystal, magiccrystal, moonstone, sunstone

Technique 13 (normal estimation): for any object whose material is in
ORGANIC, "smoothing" defaults to "auto" (edge-aware — PART 26.3's
30-degree crease threshold) if unset. For POLISHED materials, "smoothing"
defaults to "smooth" (full smoothing) if unset. Every other material is
left alone on purpose: an unset "smoothing" already means faceted/flat,
and that IS this renderer's deliberate low-poly look, not a gap to fill.

Technique 12 (Laplacian smoothing) on organic GENERATED primitives:
"generated" objects whose "generator" is sphere, tube, chain, sweep,
lathe, or superellipsoid get "smoothing":"auto" if unset — the same
edge-aware mechanism, applied by generator identity rather than material,
for the organic-shaped procedural generators specifically.

Technique 11 (region growing): objects sharing the same material are
grouped; any group of 3 or more where that material is in ORGANIC gets,
for every member that doesn't already have it:
  "colorVariation": 0.04
  "cavityShading": true
colorVariation is per-vertex tint jitter (PART 26.4); cavityShading is
fake curvature-based ambient occlusion (PART 33 B4 — documented there as
"the single cheapest, highest-value visual upgrade" for a hand-crafted
read) and is designed to combine with colorVariation rather than compete
with it. Restricted to ORGANIC materials only — a repeated metal/gem part
usually wants to read as uniform and machined, not hand-painted.

An object's own explicit "smoothing"/"colorVariation"/"cavityShading"
always wins over all of the above, at any value, including "flat"/0/false.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.5 — TECHNIQUE 6: REAL CONVEX HULL — "addConvexHull" (separate flag)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  { "addConvexHull": true, "objects": [ ... ] }
  { "addConvexHull": ["body","head"], "objects": [ ... ] }

Not part of "autoEnhance" — this is its own opt-in, because unlike 46.3/
46.4 it doesn't fill in a JSON field, it adds a new translucent proxy mesh
to the scene. "true" wraps every top-level visible mesh; an array of
object ids wraps just those. This renderer had no convex-hull computation
of any kind before this version — added via THREE's own ConvexGeometry
(three/addons/geometries/ConvexGeometry.js, alongside the other addon
imports already used throughout this file).

This runs as a POST-build step (inside finalizeSceneBuild(), after CSG,
operations, constraints, and the autoScale/autoGround transform have all
finished) rather than as a pre-build JSON transform, because a true hull
needs each mesh's REAL final world-space vertices — anything computed
earlier, from authored position/scale alone, is only ever an
approximation and can be badly wrong for anything sculpted, booleaned, or
constraint-placed. Meshes above 600 vertices are sampled (a hull only
needs extremal points, not every vertex) rather than read in full. The
hull mesh itself is translucent gray, tagged as a helper (never exported,
never individually selectable, excluded from raycasting), and is rebuilt
or removed automatically on every subsequent build like the existing
contact-shadow disc (PART 26.6).

Known limitation: an InstancedMesh — i.e. an array/scatter operation at or
