progressively, the same way an actual sculptor works in passes rather than
one global deformer.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.2 — DISPLACEMENT MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live: any object with "texturePattern" set gets a matching
procedural displacementMap for free; set "displacementAmount" (world
units) to actually push geometry along the vertex normal using it. This is
SCALAR displacement (always along the normal) — see 54.12 for true
arbitrary-direction vector displacement, which this is not.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.3 — PARAMETRIC MODELING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — this is the foundation of the entire blueprint format, not
a bolt-on feature: every primitive/generator (sphere, box, chain, sweep,
lathe, superellipsoid, and 30+ others — PART 2/9-14) is defined by named
NUMERIC PARAMETERS rather than raw vertex data, which is the actual
definition of parametric modeling (a shape driven by adjustable inputs,
regenerable from those inputs rather than hand-sculpted point by point).
Nothing to add here; naming it explicitly so it isn't mistaken for a gap.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.4 — DYNAMIC TOPOLOGY  [ASPIRATIONAL — see reasoning below]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Real dynamic topology (ZBrush/Blender's Dyntopo/multires: the mesh
RE-TRIANGULATES on the fly as you sculpt, adding density only where a
brush is actively adding detail) is fundamentally an OFFLINE AUTHORING-TOOL
algorithm, not a real-time rendering technique — there's no "renderer" to
add it to; it belongs to a sculpting application this project doesn't
have or aim to be. It's also not the same shape of problem as this
renderer's build pipeline, which generates a mesh once from parameters
rather than incrementally re-meshing under an artist's cursor.
The closest HONEST equivalent already available: "subdivisionLevel" (54.1)
sets a FIXED, uniform density per object at build time, and 54.1's new
sculptStamp adds detail only where you actually stamp — which is the
practical, achievable spirit of "topology follows where detail is added,"
even though it's a fixed build-time choice rather than a live adaptive
re-mesh. If genuinely adaptive re-triangulation (subdividing only the
triangles near a stamp, leaving the rest of the mesh at its original
density) is needed later, that's a real, larger feature to design
carefully — it's not safely retrofittable as a quick addition, since naive
partial subdivision creates T-junction cracks at the boundary between
subdivided and non-subdivided regions unless handled deliberately.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.5 — HAND-PAINTED TEXTURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW procedural pattern, same pipeline as every other "texturePattern"
value: "texturePattern": "hand_painted" (aliases: "handpainted",
"painterly"). Distinct from "stone"/"noise" (small, uniform, high-
frequency speckle): this layers a few large, irregular, low-frequency
color blotches — the uneven tonal pooling a real brush leaves — with
faint directional streaking on top (the visible drag of bristles), which
reads as painted rather than computer-generated. Works with every other
texturePattern option (textureScale, textureContrast, normalStrength,
pbrTextureSet, detailPattern — 54.14/54.15).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.6 — UV MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live — every primitive gets real UVs automatically (box
projection, picking the dominant axis per vertex from its normal), a
prerequisite this renderer has always satisfied for texturePattern/decals/
etc. to work at all. NEW in v7.8: this is now an explicit, choosable field
instead of only an automatic behind-the-scenes step —
  "uvProjection": "box" (default) | "triplanar" | "spherical" | "cylindrical"
                  | "front" | "top" | "side"
"triplanar" was previously ONLY auto-applied to CSG/heavily-twisted
geometry (54.17 covers it as its own technique). "spherical" (longitude/
latitude unwrap from the object's own bbox center) suits round props —
planets, orbs, domes, eyes — where box projection pinches visibly at the
poles. "cylindrical" (angle-around-Y + height) suits columns, bottles,
limbs, trunks. "front" / "top" / "side" (added in the same v7.8 pass, per
PART 58) are flat ORTHOGRAPHIC projections from one world axis — EVERY
vertex gets the same 2D projection regardless of which face it's on
(unlike box, which picks per-vertex by dominant normal axis), so use
these for a mostly-planar shape meant to read as a single flat reference
image mapped straight on — a sign, a card, a creature/prop authored (or
photographed, on the image-input fork) from one canonical angle — not for
a fully 3D rounded form, where they'll visibly stretch/compress on any
face not facing that axis. "front"=XY plane, "top"=XZ plane, "side"=ZY
plane; each is bbox-normalized to 0-1 independently, so no extra remap is
needed even if the LBL's own texture assumes 0-1 coverage. Omit the field
entirely to keep the exact previous behavior (box, or auto-triplanar on
flagged geometry).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.7 — DECALS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW. A top-level blueprint field, since a decal targets an object rather
than being one itself:
  "decals": [
    { "onId": "torso", "position": [0, 1.1, 0.31],
      "rotation": [0, 0, 0], "size": [0.3, 0.3, 0.3],
      "color": "#cc2222", "texturePattern": "noise", "opacity": 1 }
  ]
Uses THREE's real DecalGeometry, which projects onto the TARGET's actual
triangles/curvature — genuinely different from placing another primitive
near the surface, since it conforms to the target's own shape (an insignia
that follows a sphere's curve, a scorch mark that wraps a CSG-cut chunk's
real cut face) instead of floating as a flat independent shape that a
plain box/plane could never match. "onId" must match a real object id
(the validator errors if it doesn't — a decal with no target can't project
onto anything). "position"/"rotation"/"size" are WORLD-space, matching
DecalGeometry's own convention. "texturePattern"/"color" work the same as
any other material; omit "texturePattern" for a flat-colored decal.
Renders after CSG and every ref-based rebuild, so it's always projected
onto each target's FINAL shape, never a stale pre-boolean one.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.8 — NORMAL MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live: any "texturePattern" automatically generates a matching
procedural normalMap alongside its color map (both derived from the same
underlying height field, so the shading detail actually lines up with the
color variation instead of being unrelated noise). Tune with
"normalStrength" (how pronounced the bump is) and "normalScale" (THREE's
own normalScale multiplier). This is THE standard way real-time engines
fake high-poly surface detail on a low-poly mesh — see the 54.0 framing
note on why this, not more geometry, is the intended direction for most of
this PART.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.9 — MATCAP SHADING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW. "shading": "matcap" on any object (or "material": "matcap") swaps in
THREE's real MeshMatcapMaterial, driven by a procedurally-generated matcap
texture — no external image, same "bake a small DataTexture" approach as
every other procedural texture in this file. A matcap encodes a fully
pre-lit sphere; the material samples it by VIEW-SPACE normal instead of
computing any real lighting, so it's immune to the scene's actual lights —
the classic "clay render" / sculpting-viewport look.
  "matcapStyle": "clay" (default) | "chrome" | "pearl" | "toon" | "skin"
Genuinely different shading MODEL from the PBR materials the rest of this
renderer uses (not lit by scene lights at all), so use it deliberately —
a whole matcap-shaded character reads as a sculpting-reference/clay
maquette rather than a lit, textured final render. Combines with
"colorVariation" (vertex color tint) but not with texturePattern/PBR
fields, which don't apply to this material type.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.10 — SEPARATE MESH + TEXTURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already true architecturally, not a toggle: every object's BufferGeometry
(shape) and Material (surface appearance) are always independent objects
in this renderer, and identical texture requests are cache-deduplicated
(getProceduralTextureSet) so ten objects sharing the same "texturePattern"
+ color genuinely reuse ONE generated texture rather than building ten
copies. Documented here explicitly since it's one of the 20 requested
names, even though there's no field to set — it's how Three.js (and this
renderer) has always worked.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.11 — MICRO-POLYGONS / TESSELLATION  [ASPIRATIONAL — see reasoning]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
WebGL has no hardware tessellation shader stage at all — that's a desktop
GL4/Direct3D11-class GPU feature; Three.js/WebGL exposes no equivalent, so
genuine per-frame adaptive micro-polygon tessellation cannot be built in
this renderer, full stop, regardless of implementation effort. The honest,
real equivalent available here is CPU-side Loop subdivision at BUILD time —
exactly what "subdivisionLevel" (54.1) already is. NEW in v7.8:
"tessellationLevel" and "micropolyDetail" are accepted as aliases for
"subdivisionLevel", purely so a blueprint written with that vocabulary in
mind still does something sensible instead of the field being silently
ignored — they all feed the exact same Loop-subdivision pass.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.12 — VECTOR DISPLACEMENT
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW operation, in any object's "operations" array:
  { "type": "vectorDisplace", "params": { "strength": 0.05, "scale": 4, "seed": 7 } }
54.2's displacement is SCALAR — always along each vertex's own normal, so
it can only push a point directly outward or inward. True vector
displacement (as used in film/game sculpting pipelines to bake in
overhangs, folds, and undercuts a normal-only push can never represent)
moves each vertex by an independent 3-component offset instead. This
builds three procedural noise fields — one per axis, each with its own
random phase so they don't correlate into a single diagonal push that
would just look like scalar displacement again, only rotated — and applies
them straight to the vertex positions. "seed" makes it reproducible
(omit it to use the blueprint's global seed / true randomness, same
convention as the "noise" operation).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.13 — NANITE VIRTUALIZED GEOMETRY  [ASPIRATIONAL — see reasoning]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Nanite is Unreal Engine 5's proprietary virtualized geometry system: a
custom GPU-driven software rasterizer and streaming cluster hierarchy
purpose-built to render FILM-QUALITY, effectively unlimited-polygon-count
source art in real time. It has no WebGL/Three.js equivalent — it isn't a
technique that can be reimplemented in a weekend, it's a multi-year
proprietary rendering architecture — and, more fundamentally, its entire
purpose (render extremely HIGH-poly assets efficiently) is the opposite
goal of a low-poly renderer. Faking it would mean abandoning the low-poly
look this project exists for, not extending it.
The closest REAL, spirit-compatible, already-available mechanism: this
renderer's existing distance-based LOD system (PART 30/33 — genuine
THREE.LOD-style geometry swapping, simplifying a mesh at greater camera
distance). It's not virtualized/streamed micro-polygon geometry, but it IS
the actual, honest answer to "render appropriate detail for the viewing
distance" in a real-time WebGL context. Use "lodLevels" (existing field)
rather than expecting a "nanite" flag to do something it structurally
cannot do in this engine.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.14 — PBR TEXTURE SETS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Existing materials already used PBR properties (roughness/metalness
scalars, clearcoat/sheen/anisotropy). NEW in v7.8: a full, COHERENT
procedural texture SET, opt-in per object:
  "texturePattern": "stone", "pbrTextureSet": true,
  "roughnessBase": 0.6, "roughnessMapVariance": 0.3,
  "aoIntensity": 0.35, "metalnessBase": 0.2, "metalnessMapVariance": 0.15
Adds roughnessMap + aoMap (+ metalnessMap if "metalnessMapVariance" is
given) alongside the existing map/normalMap/displacementMap trio — and
critically, all of them are derived from the SAME underlying height field
as the color/normal maps, just read through a different tone curve, rather
than unrelated independent noise. That shared origin is what makes a
texture SET coherent rather than four random textures stacked on one
object: a crevice in the pattern is simultaneously darker (color), dented
(normal), rougher, and occluded (ao) — all in the same place, the way a
real scanned or hand-painted PBR material set is built. Fully opt-in and
additive: a "texturePattern" without "pbrTextureSet" gets exactly the old
three-texture result, unchanged.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.15 — DETAIL NORMAL / DETAIL MASK
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW. Three.js's materials expose exactly one normalMap slot — there's no
second "detail normal" input to blend at render time the way some game
engines offer. Since this renderer already bakes every texture into a
plain DataTexture up front (no custom shaders on the ordinary material
path), the practical equivalent bakes BOTH frequencies into that one map
at generation time instead:
  "texturePattern": "stone", "detailPattern": "noise",
  "detailStrength": 1.0, "detailFrequency": 6, "detailMask": "speckle"
Samples the coarse base pattern's slope AND a separate, much-higher-
frequency "detailPattern"'s slope at every texel, and Whiteout-blends the
two normals (adds the XY slope contributions, keeps Z reconstructed by
renormalizing — the standard way to layer two normal maps without washing
either one out, rather than naively averaging them). The optional
"detailMask" pattern acts as a per-texel 0-1 weight, so the fine detail
only shows up where wanted (e.g. concentrated in a pattern's own
crevices) instead of being smeared uniformly across the whole surface.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.16 — PROCEDURAL TEXTURES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live (PART 15): "texturePattern" generates map/normalMap/
displacementMap from pure math — no external images anywhere in this
renderer's material pipeline. NEW pattern in v7.8: "hand_painted" (54.5).
Existing patterns (wood, stone, brick, scales, fabric, noise,
metalbrushed, and others) are unaffected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.17 — TRIPLANAR PROJECTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already live as an automatic fallback (v7.2 B5): geometry coming out of a
CSG boolean or a heavy twist/bend/sweep gets triplanar UVs (blended by
world position instead of the bbox, so a texture keeps a consistent
physical scale on an oddly-proportioned result instead of stretching)
automatically, no field needed. NEW in v7.8: it's now also an explicit,
requestable option on ANY object, via 54.6's "uvProjection": "triplanar" —
useful for a plain, simple primitive using a PBR texture set (54.14) that
would otherwise stretch across a non-cubical bbox even without any CSG or
heavy deformation involved.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.18 — EYE SHADER WITH REFRACTION
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The "eye" material preset already had a procedural iris/pupil texture
(getEyeTexture) plus high clearcoat (a wet corneal highlight). NEW in
v7.8: a real, subtle transmission/ior layer added to the preset's
defaults, simulating actual corneal refraction — a human cornea is a
thin, mostly-transparent dome that visibly bends light passing through it
(why an iris looks subtly magnified/distorted off-angle), which flat
clearcoat alone can't reproduce, since clearcoat only adds a specular
highlight layer, it doesn't bend anything. Reuses the exact
transmission/ior/thickness mechanism already proven working for the
"glass" preset (real MeshPhysicalMaterial features, not a new code path);
"ior": 1.376 is the real physical IOR of a human cornea. Kept deliberately
subtle (0.22 transmission, thin 0.06 thickness) so it reads as a
believable wet corneal surface, not a glass marble. Any object using
"material": "eye" can dial it down — or all the way to "transmission": 0
to fully restore the pre-v7.8 flat-clearcoat-only look — since an explicit
per-object value always overrides a preset's own default.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.19 — PARALLAX OCCLUSION MAPPING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW, and the one genuinely experimental addition in this PART: normal
mapping (54.8) only fakes SHADING detail — the UV footprint sampled never
actually changes, so a shallow carved/cobbled/tiled surface still reads as
flat under a grazing view angle. Parallax occlusion mapping (POM) actually
shifts which texel gets sampled based on view direction, so nearby stones/
tiles/panels appear to occlude each other at an angle — real per-pixel
depth without adding a single triangle. Opt-in:
  "texturePattern": "stone", "parallaxOcclusion": true, "parallaxStrength": 0.04
("parallaxStrength" — how deep the illusion reads; keep it small, 0.02-0.08;
larger values distort visibly at grazing angles, an inherent limitation of
the technique, not a bug). [TIGHTENED, PART 57.7: 0.02-0.04 is the current
recommended range for production/hero assets — 0.05-0.08 remains usable
but starts showing visible texel-swimming at grazing angles.] WebGL has no
built-in per-pixel depth-offset
shading, so this is the one place in the whole renderer (outside the
existing screen-space toon outline pass) that needed a genuine custom
fragment-shader injection (onBeforeCompile) rather than a pre-baked
DataTexture or a built-in THREE material property. It's wrapped
defensively (a shader-setup failure falls back to the material's normal,
non-POM appearance rather than breaking the object) and never touches any
material that doesn't explicitly opt in. Reuses the object's own
procedural displacementMap as the height source, so it stays visually
consistent with that same object's normal map and scalar displacement.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.20 — EYE TRACKING RIG
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NEW, and a genuinely different kind of feature from the static "lookAt"/
"aim" fields (PART 33 #14, and the dynamic-anchor aim vector) — both of
those resolve ONCE at build time and never move again. This is a real
per-FRAME rig, re-solved every frame in the render loop, so the eyes
visibly follow a moving target instead of staring at one fixed point
forever:
  "eyeTrack": true
  "eyeTrack": { "target": "camera", "maxAngle": 30 }
"target": "camera" (default — the character appears to track the viewer
as the model is orbited), or a literal [x,y,z] world point, or another
object's id (an eye that tracks a held prop, a second character, etc.).
"maxAngle" (default 30°) clamps how far the eye can swivel from its own
AUTHORED REST ORIENTATION (whatever pose/joint/aim it already had at build
time) — so it reads as a believable eye-track constraint with realistic
limits, never spinning to face fully backward if the target passes behind
it. Put this on the actual eyeball mesh (typically "material": "eye"),
not on the head — the head stays where it's posed; only the eye itself
swivels within its socket.

================================================================================
PART 55 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: POSITIONING-INTEGRITY AUDIT (5 REAL FIXES)
================================================================================
PARTs 36-45 already did a major pass on limb/prop positioning. This is a
follow-up audit specifically of the ROTATION/ORIENTATION math in
buildScene()/applyConstraints() — traced line by line against the actual
renderer source, not just re-stated authoring advice — prompted by a
report that positioning still wasn't fully right after that pass. Two of
the five are pure ordering bugs; two are mathematically demonstrable
errors in rotation math (verified against independently-derived ground-
truth matrices before shipping, not just reasoned about); one is a missing
validator warning for a known silent-override risk. All five are pure bug
fixes — nothing about the blueprint schema changed, so no existing,
already-working blueprint is affected; blueprints that were quietly
hitting one of these bugs will now render correctly instead.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
55.1 — ASYMMETRY TILT COULD BE SILENTLY ERASED BY lookAt/aim
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
blueprint.asymmetry's "_tilt" suffix (PART 34-R.7/26.8) added its rotation
immediately after an object's explicit "rotation" field was applied — but
inheritRotation, the dynamic-anchor aim vector, top-level "aim", and by-ID
"lookAt" all run AFTER that point and FULLY OVERWRITE rotation (a
quaternion copy or a fresh lookAt() call), silently erasing whatever tilt
had just been added. This is an entirely ordinary combination for a rigged
character — e.g. a head riding a joint AND using "lookAt" to face the
camera, with asymmetry adding a cosmetic head_tilt — so the bug was
reachable through completely normal, encouraged authoring patterns, not
just an edge case.
FIX: the tilt is now only RECORDED when each object is first processed,
and actually applied in one unified pass at the very end of buildScene(),
after every other rotation-setting mechanism (immediate and deferred
alike, including the by-ID lookAt pass and the ref-resolution rebuild
pass) has already run. It is now structurally impossible for any
combination of features to erase it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
55.2 — MIRRORED PARTS COULD TILT THE WRONG WAY ("symmetrical"/"mirrorFace")
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Mirroring an object's ROTATION across a plane isn't just "copy the
rotation" — reflecting a rotation matrix requires negating specific
components, and which ones depends on the mirror axis. The "symmetrical"
constraint (used for ordinary left/right character mirroring) and
"mirrorFace" both only negated the yaw component and silently left the
other two untouched. Verified by conjugating the actual XYZ-order rotation
matrix by each reflection matrix (the standard M·R·M test) across 2000
randomized angles before shipping the fix — 0 mismatches against the
corrected version, versus consistent, demonstrable mismatches against the
old one whenever the source part had any roll/tilt on it.
Concretely: any left/right-mirrored part that ALSO had a tilt on it (an
asymmetry _tilt, PART 34-R.7's own headline feature; a hand-posed lean;
anything from 54.1's new sculptStamp used asymmetrically) mirrored to the
WRONG side of that tilt — both copies leaning the same way instead of
mirroring, the kind of subtly-wrong pose a person notices without being
able to say exactly why.
FIX: "symmetrical" now correctly negates both the yaw and roll components
for its (always left/right) mirror. "mirrorFace" now correctly negates the
matching pair of components for whichever axis (or both axes, for the
diagonal/4-way case) it's mirroring across.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
55.3 — RADIAL ARRAYS COULD ORIENT COPIES SLIGHTLY WRONG (radialRepeat/radialSymmetry)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Placing N copies in a ring updated orientation by overwriting a single
Euler rotation component ("clone.rotation.y = mesh.rotation.y + angle")
while copying the other two straight from the source. That's only
equivalent to "rigidly rotate this part's whole orientation around the
ring's axis" when the source has ZERO rotation on the other two axes —
rotations about different axes don't commute, so Euler components don't
compose by simple addition once more than one axis is involved. A
radially-repeated part that itself leans or tilts (ordinary for spokes,
petals, teeth, prongs authored with a slight outward or forward angle) got
a subtly wrong lean on every copy but the first.
FIX: both constraints now build the rotation step as a quaternion around
the true array axis and premultiply the source's actual quaternion,
rigidly rotating the whole existing orientation regardless of what it
started as. Verified against independently-derived matrix rotation across
2000 randomized cases (0 mismatches) before shipping.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
55.4 — MISSING VALIDATOR WARNING: explicit "position" + attachTo
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
solvePosition() has always had a strict, intentional priority order —
explicit "position" wins over attachTo/parent/placement, no exceptions,
documented as such. The validator already warned about ONE way to
accidentally trigger this (a resolving "joint" field, which silently
WRITES obj.position, combined with attachTo) — but not the more common,
more direct case: an object with a literal, hand-typed "position" field
left in place (a leftover from an earlier edit, or copied from another
object as a starting point) ALSO combined with attachTo. Same silent-
override behavior, same risk, no warning.
FIX: the validator now warns on this combination too, with the same
"remove one or the other" guidance as the existing joint+attachTo check.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
55.5 — REF-RESOLUTION VS. BY-ID lookAt ORDERING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A smaller ordering issue caught during the same audit: the deferred
by-ID "lookAt" pass used to resolve BEFORE the deferred ref-resolution
pass (for objects using "ref:" strings in position/scale/rotation). An
object needing BOTH — a ref-driven field AND a lookAt target — got its
lookAt orientation silently discarded when the ref-resolution pass ran
afterward and reset rotation from scratch.
FIX: ref-resolution now runs first, so lookAt-by-id (and 55.1's final
asymmetry-tilt pass after it) each get to have the last, correct word over
whichever mesh they target.

================================================================================
PART 56 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: CONTRADICTION AUDIT (5 REAL FIXES)

A pass specifically looking for places where this document contradicts

56.1 — OUTPUT-FORMAT REMINDER CONTRADICTED THIS FILE'S OWN CORE PURPOSE
  The single highest-impact fix in this pass. PART 32 exists specifically

PART 57 — v7.8 PIXEL-PERFECT FIDELITY PASS (per pixel_perfect_3d_techniques.txt,
           Final Master Edition, 2026-07-21)
================================================================================
A companion reference file, pixel_perfect_3d_techniques.txt, consolidates the
highest-impact techniques from PARTs 6/16/19/25/26/34-R/37/39/40/54 into one
5-section checklist for pixel-perfect fidelity and high-fidelity structural
precision. Every mechanism it names already exists in the renderer (verified
directly against low_poly_3d.html — nothing below is a new field); this
PART's job is to (a) state the few genuinely underdocumented USAGE PATTERNS
it calls for — chiefly 57.1's pixel grid and 57.5's nested-eye pattern — and
(b) pin down exact recommended ranges where an earlier PART only documented
a field's full technical range, not the narrower part of that range that
actually looks good. Cross-references below point at the PART documenting
the underlying mechanism in full; this PART does not repeat that mechanism,
only the fidelity-focused rule for when/how much to use it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.1 — THE "PIXEL GRID" TECHNIQUE (new pattern; mechanism = PART 16)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
For ultra-detailed focal micro-features — an iris's fine radial detail, a
small emblem/logo, a grille, a cluster of tiny scratches or rivets — that
are too fine for one primitive or one texture pattern to sell convincingly,
build a GRID of tiny cube primitives with the "generate" macro (PART 16)
instead of reaching for a bigger flat panel:

  {
    "generate": {
      "count": 64, "as": "i",
      "template": {
        "id":    "px_{{i}}",
        "type":  "box",
        "scale": [0.04, 0.04, 0.04],
        "position": [
          "{{ (i % 8) * 0.045 - 0.16 }}",
          "{{ Math.floor(i / 8) * 0.045 }}",
          0
        ],
        "colorVariation": 0.06,
        "toneVariant": "{{ (i % 8 + Math.floor(i/8)) % 3 === 0 ? 'light' : ((i % 8 + Math.floor(i/8)) % 3 === 1 ? 'mid' : 'dark') }}",
        "attachTo": "chest_emblem_panel", "placement": "front"
      }
    }
  }

Each "pixel" is a ~0.04-unit cube; count/8 gives the grid's column width (8
columns above, ~0.36 units wide total — sized for a chest emblem/logo/
instrument-panel-scale focal feature). SCALE DOWN both the cube size and
the spacing together for a smaller feature (e.g. an iris's fine detail
inside an eyeball that's itself only ~0.08-0.15 units across calls for
~0.006-0.01-unit cubes, not 0.04 — always size the grid relative to the
actual feature it sits on, never at a fixed absolute size). The point of
varying "toneVariant" across a small modulus (light/mid/dark, not literally
random) alongside a modest per-cube "colorVariation" (0.04-0.08 — 57.3) is
the staggered, multi-toned look of a genuinely high-resolution render,
deliberately not a perfectly uniform mosaic. attachTo + placement (or
sideOf/sideToward — 57.4 — if the parent surface is rotated/curved) keeps
the whole grid flush to its parent surface as one unit.
This is a focal-detail tool, not a general-purpose one: reserve it for the
few features on a hero subject that the composition actually invites a
close look at (irises, chest emblems, small instrument panels).
texturePattern/decals (54.5/54.7/54.16) remain the right, far cheaper
tool for anything larger than a few resolvable cells, or on a surface
that isn't a focal point.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.2 — box_subdivided + subdivisionLevel: REQUIRED FOR CHARACTER CORES
       AND HERO PROPS (mechanism = PART 25, 54.1)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Promote from "available option" to a hard rule for two specific categories
of object: (1) a character's head/torso/core body mass, and (2) a hero or
focal prop — the one object the composition is actually built around. For
both:
  - Use "generator": "box_subdivided" (or another subdivided/generated
    primitive — sphere/capsule/etc. — never a raw single-segment "type":
    "box") as the base shape.
  - Set "params.round" to 0.02-0.05 (PART 25 documents the field's full
    0-0.3 technical range; 0.02-0.05 is the part of that range that reads
    as "catches highlights smoothly" rather than "fully rounded blob" —
    stay under it and edges look sharp/plasticky, go much over it and the
    faceted low-poly silhouette disappears).
  - Pair it with "smoothing": "auto" so those rounded corners actually get
    smooth-shaded (PART 26.3/54.1) — round without smoothing still flat-
    shades every facet, which mutes most of the effect "round" exists to
    create.
  - For a hero prop specifically (not usually needed on a character core,
    which gets its density from part count instead), consider adding
    "subdivisionLevel": 1 on top for genuine extra resolvable geometry,
    not just smoothed shading (rarely go to 2 — see 54.1/54.11 on why
    this renderer's Loop-subdivision is the honest ceiling, not real-time
    hardware tessellation).
Secondary/background/set-dressing objects are explicitly NOT required to
do this — spend the extra density budget on what the composition is
actually about, per PART 53.6's model-integrity framing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.3 — colorVariation + cavityShading: THE STANDARD SURFACE PAIRING
       (mechanism = PART 26.4 colorVariation / PART 34's B4 cavityShading)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
colorVariation's full technical range is 0-0.25 (PART 26.4), but the range
that actually reads as hand-painted low-poly rather than flat plastic (one
end) or dirty/noisy (the other) is 0.04-0.08 — every full worked example
elsewhere in this document already lands in exactly that range; treat it
as the rule those examples were quietly already following, not a new
constraint. Pair it with "cavityShading": true (or an explicit 0.3-0.5 for
a stronger read) on anything with real creases, joints, or muscle/panel-
seam definition — skin, folded fabric, mechanical panel lines. The two
combine additively (cavityShading darkens on top of colorVariation's
existing per-face noise, never replacing it), which is what produces the
"hand-painted AND lit" look this section of the guide is describing —
colorVariation alone reads as noise; cavityShading alone reads as flat
color with dark seams; together they read as a considered material.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.4 — sideOf/sideToward STAYS MANDATORY FOR ROTATED/CURVED PARENTS
       (restates PART 37 — no new mechanism)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Included here only because it's precisely the "prevent surface details
from drifting" concern the pixel-perfect guide raises. PART 37 already
makes this a RULE, not an option: any object attaching to a parent with a
non-zero "rotation", or to a chain/tube segment, must use sideOf/sideToward
rather than plain "placement". Applies equally to 57.1's pixel grids and
54.7's decals — anything meant to sit flush on a surface that isn't
guaranteed axis-aligned.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.5 — NESTED FEATURE DEPTH FOR HERO EYES (new recommendation; mechanism =
       ordinary attachTo nesting + PART 19's <0.2-scale local-offset rule)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The "eye" material preset (54.18) bakes iris/pupil/sclera into ONE flat
procedural texture on a single sphere — a good, cheap default for any
background character, secondary figure, or anything small-in-frame.
For a HERO character specifically (the actual main subject, face reading
as a meaningful part of the composition), build the eye as genuinely
separate nested primitives instead of one textured sphere:
  eye_socket (a recessed/concave base, or simply the head geometry's own
    natural socket) -> eyeball (a sphere, "material":"eye" or a plain
    sclera color, attachTo the socket) -> pupil (a small dark sphere or
    disc, attachTo the eyeball, "placement":"front" or sideOf/sideToward
    front).
This gives genuine parallax between the three layers and a real cast
shadow from the socket onto the eyeball as the camera orbits — depth a
single flat-textured sphere cannot produce, at the cost of 2-3 extra
objects per eye. Put "eyeTrack" (54.20/57.6) on the eyeball specifically,
never the socket. Rule of thumb: flat "eye" material for anything that
isn't the focal subject; nested eye_socket/eyeball/pupil for the hero.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.6 — EYE TRACKING DEFAULTS TO ON FOR HERO SUBJECTS (restates 54.20)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Any hero character's eyeball mesh(es) should default to "eyeTrack": true
(target:"camera" is the default target and is almost always right for a
model meant to be orbited/viewed) unless the character has an explicit
lookAt/aim already posing it to look at something specific in-scene. An
untracked eye on an otherwise pixel-perfect face reads as dead/glassy the
moment the viewer orbits the model — a small omission with a
disproportionately large effect on whether the face reads as "alive."

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.7 — PARALLAX OCCLUSION MAPPING: TIGHTENED STRENGTH RANGE
       [CORRECTS the outer edge of 54.19's stated range]
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
54.19 gives parallaxStrength's safe range as 0.02-0.08. Tightened
recommendation: 0.02-0.04. Above roughly 0.05 the fixed 10-step search POM
actually runs (54.19's own implementation note) starts visibly texel-
swimming at grazing angles rather than reading as extra depth — an
artifact, not a stronger effect. 0.04 (also this field's engine default
when omitted) is the practical ceiling for a production/hero asset;
reserve 0.05-0.08 only for a deliberately exaggerated, stylized deep-
carved look where the swimming itself is an acceptable trade.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.8 — ASYMMETRY TILT MAGNITUDE (restates PART 34-R.7/26.8's own examples
       as an explicit rule)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The mechanism (blueprint.asymmetry's _tilt/_drop/_lift/_shift suffixes,
additive to whatever rotation/position a joint's objects already have —
PART 34-R.7) is unchanged. Stated explicitly for the first time: the
magnitude that reads as "alive, hand-crafted" rather than "visibly broken"
is 2-3° for any _tilt suffix, and roughly 0.02-0.05 world units for
_drop/_lift/_shift on a human-scale character (scale proportionally for a
much larger/smaller subject). This isn't a new number — PART 22/34-R/40's
own full worked examples already use exactly this range (e.g.
"head_tilt": 3, "shoulder_l_drop": 0.03-0.04) — it just wasn't previously
written down as the rule those examples were following. Meaningfully
higher reads as an injury or manufacturing defect, not natural asymmetry.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.9 — VALIDATION IS THE LAST STEP, NOT AN OPTIONAL ONE (restates PART 39)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Restated here only because the pixel-perfect guide singles it out as its
own closing point: the workflow is not complete until the generated script
has actually been pasted into low_poly_3d.html and the validation log
reviewed. validateBlueprintSchema/scanForBadRefs/postSchemaCheck exist
specifically to catch SILENT failures — a bad attachTo/sideOf/lookAt/joint
id that quietly falls back to a default position or rotation instead of
erroring, an unrecognized material/type/generator name that silently
falls back to a generic default look, a missing proportion-declaration
field — none of which crash the render or produce any visible error in
the 3D view itself; they just make the result subtly, silently wrong in
exactly the way a quick glance won't catch. PART 39 already establishes
reading the log as mandatory workflow; this item exists only to connect
that same requirement explicitly to fidelity/pixel-perfection, not to add
a new rule.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
57.10 — MIDTONE SAMPLING (restates the intro's / PART 49-50's existing rule)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Already established (see this document's opening color-sampling guidance
and PART 49/50): every base color written into the blueprint should be
sampled from the MIDTONE of its region in the reference image, never a
specular-highlight or core-shadow pixel — both run lighter/darker than the
material's true hue and, sampled directly, bake one lighting condition
from the reference photo into what's supposed to be a flat, relightable
base color that this renderer's own lights will shade correctly on its
own. Restated here because the pixel-perfect guide frames it as
foundational to the whole palette, not just one checklist item among many:
get this wrong and 57.3's colorVariation/cavityShading/toneVariant are all
perturbing an already-wrong base color.

================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 58 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: RENDER-QUALITY GUARANTEES (already-live in the
           renderer, previously undocumented here — source:
           lbl_patch_1_quickwins.txt, cross-checked against low_poly_3d.html)
================================================================================
A "patch" file describing 4 renderer quick-wins (explicit color-space flags,
style-aware tone mapping, orthographic project-from-view UV, emissive
soft-clamp) was cross-checked line-by-line against the actual current
renderer source, per this project's usual audit-before-documenting workflow.
All 4 were already implemented and shipped (as PART 55 items 18-21 in the
renderer's own code comments — a different, code-local numbering than this
document's PART 55, which covers something else entirely; don't conflate
the two) — none of the patch's literal snippets should be pasted in, since
that would duplicate working code. This PART's only job is to document the
already-live BEHAVIOR the blueprint author needs to know about, since none
of it was previously written down here. No blueprint schema field changed
except 54.6's new "front"/"top"/"side" uvProjection values, already folded
into that section above.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
58.1 — COLOR-SPACE CORRECTNESS IS GUARANTEED, NOT AUTHOR-FACING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every procedurally-generated texture is explicitly flagged sRGB (color:
albedo/emissive maps) or linear/NoColorSpace (data: normal/roughness/
metalness/ao/displacement/detail maps) at creation time — this was already
true per-map before this pass; nothing to author or request here. Mentioned
only so it's clear WHY normal/roughness/metalness/ao maps never look
washed-out or double-darkened regardless of texturePattern/pbrTextureSet
combination: the renderer never relies on a THREE.js default that could
silently change across versions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
58.2 — TONE MAPPING IS PER-STYLE, NOT ONE GLOBAL CURVE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer picks a different THREE.js tone-mapping operator per
meta.style (48.1's exact style list) instead of applying one hardcoded
curve to every render:
  NoToneMapping      → voxel, flat-low-poly, isometric-flat, wireframe,
                        x-ray, anime-cel, toon
  ACESFilmicToneMapping → pbr-realistic, photogrammetry, smooth-low-poly
  LinearToneMapping  → painterly, low-poly-painterly
  CineonToneMapping  → stylized, papercraft, chibi
PRACTICAL EFFECT for color authoring: for the flat/hard-edged styles (top
row), the exact hex you write in baseColor/emissive renders with NO
filmic roll-off or desaturation — what you author is what you get, so
don't over-compensate a flat-low-poly/voxel/anime-cel color for an
expected "camera" darkening that won't happen. For pbr-realistic/
photogrammetry/smooth-low-poly, ACES roll-off DOES apply (as it always
has), so very bright/saturated baseColor values will read slightly
desaturated and rolled-off toward the highlight, same as before this
pass — no change for that group. Getting meta.style right (PART 48) now
therefore also determines the color-rendering PIPELINE, not just the
shading model — one more reason 48.2's style-identification step must run
before any other authoring decision.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
58.3 — PROJECT-FROM-VIEW UV MODES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Covered in full at 54.6 above ("front"/"top"/"side"). Cross-referenced
here only so this PART's summary of the source patch is complete.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
58.4 — EMISSIVE INTENSITY HARD CEILING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Covered in full at the emissiveIntensity rule-of-thumb earlier in this
document. Cross-referenced here only so this PART's summary of the source
patch is complete: soft warning above 2.5, hard clamp at 5.0 — 5.0 is the
real ceiling, not just a style guideline.

================================================================================
PART 59 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: TEXTURING / FIDELITY / LIGHTING GUARANTEES
           (already-live in the renderer, previously undocumented here —
           source: lbl_patch_2_medium.txt, cross-checked against
           low_poly_3d.html)
================================================================================
A "patch" file describing 3 renderer medium-features (image-to-UV painting,
reference-silhouette IoU, auto env-map for pbr-realistic) was cross-checked
line-by-line against the actual current renderer source, per this
project's usual audit-before-documenting workflow. All 3 were already
implemented and shipped (as PART 55 items 22/23/24 in the renderer's own
code comments — a different, code-local numbering than this document's
PART 55, which covers something else entirely; don't conflate the two —
see PART 58's opening note for the same caveat about the prior patch)
— the patch's literal snippets should NOT be pasted in, since the shipped
implementations are more complete than the patch's generic versions (per-
object override roles, correct color-space per slot, "forces on, never
off" semantics, and a WebGLRenderTarget-based IoU pass rather than a
live-canvas screenshot). This PART's only job is to document the
already-live BEHAVIOR the blueprint author needs to know about.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
59.1 — IMAGE-TO-UV PAINTING ("textureImage" / "useMasterTexture")
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
meta.textureImage may be set to a Data URL (a "master map" — for this
image-input fork, this can be the source reference image itself, or a
separately painted/uploaded map, via the Prompt Builder's "Master
texture image" field). Any object opts in to it with
"useMasterTexture": true — nothing changes for objects that omit the
field, same backward-compatible opt-in pattern as autoEnhance/
outlineMode. An object may ALSO supply its own "textureImage" Data URL
to override just that one part (e.g. a decal on a single panel) without
affecting the rest of the model, plus an optional "textureImageRole"
(default "map"; also accepts "emissiveMap", "roughnessMap",
"normalMap") to target a different material slot. Color space is
handled automatically per role — "map"/"emissiveMap" are read as sRGB
color, everything else (normal/roughness/metalness/ao) is read as
linear data — so nothing needs to be declared about color space when
authoring these fields.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
59.2 — REFERENCE SILHOUETTE IoU (PART 51.2 item 4 / SILHOUETTE MATCH)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
When the source image is attached in the renderer (Compare mode / the
Prompt Builder's reference library, PART 52), the fidelity gate's
SILHOUETTE MATCH check now runs for real instead of staying permanently
"skipped": the current build is rendered to a 64x64 alpha grid, the
attached reference image is sampled to the same grid (falling back to a
luminance threshold if the reference has no real transparency, e.g. a
plain photo), and the two grids are compared by intersection-over-union.
Pass threshold is IoU ≥ 50%, surfaced as a percentage in the fidelity
chip/val-log/Inspector Fidelity tab. See the corrected note at PART 51.2
item 4 above.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
59.3 — AUTO ENV-MAP FOR pbr-realistic
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The renderer's procedural environment map (RoomEnvironment + PMREM,
generated once at boot) previously only turned on via the manual HDRI
toolbar button. meta.style: "pbr-realistic" now auto-enables it, since
PBR metals/glass read as flat gray without image-based lighting. This
follows a "forces on, never off" rule — the same precedent as
OUTLINE_FORCE_STYLES (PART 48.3): choosing pbr-realistic turns HDRI on
if it wasn't already, but does not turn it off for any other style, so a
user who enabled it manually for a non-PBR style keeps that choice.
Nothing to author here — this is pure renderer behavior driven off
meta.style, not a new blueprint field.

================================================================================
PART 60 — v7.8 ADDITIONS: PRIMITIVE-COUNT SUGGESTION, LIBRARY THUMBNAILS,
           EMISSIVE MASKS (already-live in the renderer, previously
           undocumented here — source: lbl_patch_3_bigfeatures.txt,
           cross-checked against low_poly_3d.html)
================================================================================
A "patch" file describing 3 renderer big-features (client-side primitive-
count suggestion + an honest link to real AI reconstruction, reference-
library thumbnails, emissive masks) was cross-checked line-by-line against
the actual current renderer source, per this project's usual audit-before-
documenting workflow. All 3 were already implemented and shipped (as
PART 55 items 25/26/27 in the renderer's own code comments — a different,
code-local numbering than this document's PART 55, which covers something
else entirely; don't conflate the two — see PART 58's opening note for the
same caveat about the prior patches) — the patch's literal snippets should
NOT be pasted in, since the shipped implementations differ from the
patch's generic versions in real, load-bearing ways (see 60.1 and 60.3
below for the specifics). This PART's only job is to document the
already-live BEHAVIOR the blueprint author needs to know about. Only ONE
new blueprint schema field exists across all three items — "emissiveMask"
(60.3); the other two are Prompt Builder / renderer UI behavior with
nothing to author in the blueprint itself.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
60.1 — PRIMITIVE-COUNT SUGGESTION (PROMPT BUILDER UI, NOT A BLUEPRINT FIELD)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
When the reference image attached in the Prompt Builder (the mandatory input
on this image-input fork) is analyzed, a color-region-counting heuristic
(32×32 downsample, 4-bit-per-channel color quantization, distinct-bucket
count, clamped to 3-40) suggests a rough "how many distinct objects/parts"
number, shown as inline text under the reference thumbnail: "Rough estimate
from color-region counting: this reference might need around N distinct
objects/parts — a starting point, not a target." This is NOT AI/ML shape
reconstruction and runs no inference of any kind — it's a local pixel-
counting proxy, and the copy is deliberately hedged as an estimate rather than
a target; don't treat the number as a hard object-count requirement when
writing the blueprint. The same panel links out to the public TripoSR demo
(triposr.com) for anyone who wants real AI-driven image→3D reconstruction,
with instructions to download the resulting GLB and drop it into this
renderer's existing "Upload 3D" button — no network call happens inside this
renderer itself, consistent with its local-only design (PART 1). The patch's
own reference implementation used a flood- fill region-count algorithm with a
different threshold table (1-2 up to 8-12+ across six bands); the shipped
version instead does a simpler distinct-quantized-color-bucket count clamped
to a 3-40 range — same intent, different math, so don't assume the patch's
specific thresholds apply. Nothing to author here; this never touches the
blueprint schema — the number is a human-facing hint the author can use,
ignore, or override however they like when deciding how many objects to write.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
60.2 — REFERENCE LIBRARY THUMBNAILS (PART 52.5)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every blueprint saved to the reference library
(saveBlueprintToReferenceLibrary(), PART 52.5 — fires automatically after
any successful build where both meta.style and meta.category are set)
now also captures a 256×256 PNG snapshot of the live canvas at save time
and stores it on the entry as "thumbnail" (a Data URL). The library panel
(Prompt Builder → Reference library) renders these as a click-to-restore
visual gallery instead of a plain text list, keyed by the same
`${style}__${category}` bucket as before, most-recent-first, capped at 5
per bucket exactly as before this pass. Best-effort only: canvas capture
can fail (e.g. a tainted canvas from a cross-origin texture), in which
case the entry is still saved, just without a thumbnail — don't assume
every library entry has one, especially entries saved before this pass
shipped. Nothing to author here either; the thumbnail field lives entirely
inside the localStorage-backed library entry, never inside a blueprint
you write or the renderer reads back in.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
60.3 — EMISSIVE MASK ("emissiveMask") — THE ONE NEW BLUEPRINT FIELD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Addresses the paper's "areas for purple emissive eyes can be reserved on a
separate, dedicated emissive texture map" case: previously "emissive"/
"emissiveIntensity" on an object made the WHOLE material glow evenly, with
no way to reserve just a sub-region (e.g. only the eyes, only a rune
inscription). An object may now set:

  "emissiveMask": { "hue": 280, "hueTolerance": 40, "minSaturation": 0.35 }

— hue is a standard 0-360 HSL hue angle (280 = purple, the default; NOT a
0-1 normalized value — the patch's own UI mockup used a 0-1 slider
default 0.83, which does NOT match the shipped field's units, so convert
if porting any UI copy from that patch). hueTolerance is the +/- degree
window around that hue (default 40); minSaturation (0-1) is the minimum
HSL saturation a pixel must have to qualify (default 0.35). The mask is
built from a SOURCE IMAGE resolved in this priority order: the object's
own "emissiveMaskImage" (a dedicated Data URL, if you want the mask
painted separately from the visible texture) → else the object's own
"textureImage" → else the blueprint-wide "meta.textureImage" IF the
object also has "useMasterTexture": true. If none of those resolve, the
mask silently does not apply and a console warning is logged — set
"emissiveMaskImage" explicitly if the object has no other texture image
to fall back on. Every pixel in that source image within hueTolerance of
the target hue AND at or above minSaturation becomes a WHITE (fully-
emissive) texel in a generated black/white mask; everything else is
black. The mask is then multiplied directly into the material's
totalEmissiveRadiance — the black region gets ZERO emissive contribution
REGARDLESS of how high "emissiveIntensity" is set, and the "emissive"/
"emissiveIntensity" fields still control the base glow color/strength for
whatever the mask lets through, exactly as before; emissiveMask only
gates WHICH pixels of that base glow are allowed to show. One hard
prerequisite: the material must already have a UV-consuming map (map,
normalMap, or roughnessMap) present, since vUv is only compiled into the
shader when one of those exists — an object with no texture at all and no
useMasterTexture opt-in cannot receive an emissive mask; it silently
no-ops with a console warning rather than shipping a broken shader.
Application is asynchronous (the source image must decode first), so
there is a brief window after a build where a masked object may render
without its emissive glow before the mask resolves — this is expected,
not a bug to work around in the blueprint. Build/render logic itself
needs no author-side change beyond writing the emissiveMask object; it is
picked up automatically inside buildMaterial() for every object that sets
it.


================================================================================
PART 61 — v7.9 ADDITIONS: ORGANIC SCULPTED-FORM DECISION FRAMEWORK +
           MULTI-BALL LIMB TECHNIQUE (source: LBL_TECHNIQUES_AND_SOLUTIONS.txt,
           distilled from direct build experience matching a soft sculpted
           humanoid reference; cross-checked against PARTs 6, 9, 10, 12, 26,
           40 — this PART adds a decision layer and one genuinely new
           sub-technique inside PART 12, it does not change any renderer code
           or field)
================================================================================
Everything in this PART is semantic/workflow guidance layered on top of
fields and rules already verified elsewhere in this document (PARTs 6, 9,
10, 12, 26, 40 below). Read this PART FIRST whenever the reference is a
soft, sculpted, continuous figure (a person, an animal, a blob, a melted
or claymation-style form) — it exists because that specific case has a
sharp, easy-to-miss failure mode that the individual PARTs it draws on
don't call out on their own.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
61.1 — THE CORE PROBLEM: SEPARATE PRIMITIVES NEVER MERGE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
For a SOFT/SCULPTED/CONTINUOUS reference, the intuitive approach — a
sphere for the head, a capsule for the torso, capsules for the arms and
legs, positioned to touch — is wrong no matter how carefully the
proportions are tuned. Every primitive the renderer builds is an
INDEPENDENT MESH with its own closed surface. Positioning two of them so
they touch or overlap does not fuse their geometry; it just puts two
separate surfaces near each other. The result reads as a "snowman": a
head balanced on a body, with limbs that look glued on, a visible seam at
every junction, and (at low "detail") visible polygon facets on any
sphere/capsule — all symptoms of the same root cause, not independent
bugs to patch one at a time. Adding a neck cylinder to bridge a
head/body gap does not fix this either — it's a third separate
primitive, hiding the seam behind a small tube rather than removing it.
The only fixes are the three ACTUAL-merge techniques in 61.2 below; no
amount of position/scale tuning on separate primitives gets there.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
61.2 — WHICH MERGE TECHNIQUE, AND WHEN (decision layer over PARTs 10/12/40)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Three techniques in this document actually produce continuous/merged
geometry. Pick per-reference, not reflexively:

  Q1. Is the subject ONE continuous sculpted mass (skin/fur/clay reads as
      a single unbroken surface, no hard panel lines)?
        YES → sdf_blob (PART 12), with multi-ball limbs if it has
              clearly defined long limbs (61.3 below).
        NO  → go to Q2.
  Q2. Is the subject hard-surface (needs to read as one mesh but is
      built from distinct-shaped parts — a car body, furniture, a
      mechanical housing)?
        YES → CSG boolean union (PART 10) on the parts that must fuse.
        NO  → go to Q3.
  Q3. Does the subject have clearly defined long limbs but an otherwise
      separable body (a stylized character where the torso can
      reasonably stay its own primitive, but each arm/leg must read as
      one unbroken tube from socket to tip)?
        YES → the "chain" generator (PART 40) for each limb, attached to
              the body via attachTo/placement (PART 41). This gives
              seamless limb-internal continuity but the limb is still a
              mesh separate from the torso, joined at a visible boundary
              you manage with sizing/overlap (PART 26.5) — use this only
              when the reference actually shows that kind of separable
              read (a jointed toy, armor over a body), not for skin-to-
              skin continuous forms, which want Q1's sdf_blob instead.
        NO  → ordinary separate primitives are fine as-is; use "detail" 3
              and a matte/smooth material (61.5 / PART 26.3) for a clean
              sculpted look without needing any merge technique at all.

CAVEAT — RIG-TEST / EXTERNAL-VIEWER EXPORT (see PART 63): Q1's sdf_blob
and Q2's CSG boolean union are both genuine single-mesh techniques by
design — that's WHY they read as seamless. Applied to a FEW parts (one
limb's balls, a car body's 4-5 panels) that's exactly the intended,
correct use. Do NOT extend either technique to fuse the ENTIRE model —
head, torso, all limbs — into one blob/union chain just because the
model happens to be organic or hard-surface overall. A whole-model
single mesh is invisible in THIS renderer (it still looks seamless) but
means the exported .glb has exactly one mesh/group, which fails
external rig-test viewers outright (PART 63) and defeats per-part
picking. Scope Q1/Q2 to the smallest region that actually needs to read
as fused — one limb, one panel cluster — and keep the rest (head vs.
torso vs. other limbs vs. props) as genuinely separate top-level mesh
entries per PART 63's multi-part requirement.

If the render still shows visible facets after picking the right branch:
bump "detail" to 3 (primitives) or "resolution" to 48 (sdf_blob) — see
61.5 — before assuming the technique choice itself was wrong.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
61.3 — THE MISSING PIECE: MULTI-BALL LIMBS INSIDE A SINGLE sdf_blob
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
This extends PART 12's "balls" array with the one insight that makes
sdf_blob actually work for a continuous figure with recognizable arms
and legs, rather than a shapeless puddle:

  ONE BALL PER LIMB PRODUCES A WIDE LUMP, NOT A LIMB.
  MULTIPLE SMALL BALLS IN A VERTICAL LINE ALONG THE LIMB PRODUCES A LIMB.

A single ball placed at the shoulder just blends into the body ball as
a rounded bump the size of that one ball's field of influence — there is
no length to it. To get an actual arm or leg out of the same single
continuous mesh:
  - Place 3-5 SMALL balls in a line from the socket (shoulder/hip) down
    to the extremity (hand/foot), each one a little further along the
    limb.
  - Keep each ball's "strength" small (roughly 0.08-0.12) — much lower
    than the 0.3-0.9 range PART 12 gives for a general blobby-creature
    ball, because here the ball is one segment of a long thin shape, not
    the whole shape.
  - Let neighboring balls in the chain overlap slightly so marching
    cubes reads them as one continuous tube rather than a string of
    separate bumps.
  - Taper the last ball in each chain slightly smaller (roughly
    0.07-0.08) for a natural narrowing hand/foot end.
  - Use the same "subtract" value (roughly 10-14) across the whole
    figure's balls for a consistent blend tightness, per PART 12's
    general organic-merge guidance.
Apply the same pattern to legs (typically 3 balls: hip, knee, foot) as
to arms (typically 4 balls: shoulder, upper arm, forearm, hand). A body
built this way is ONE "sdf_blob" object with one "balls" array — a head
ball (or a head ball + small neck ball for a smoother transition), one
or two body balls, and one vertical chain of small balls per limb — not
five separate primitives and not five separate sdf_blob objects.

EXAMPLE — one continuous humanoid figure, head/body/2 arms/2 legs, as a
single sdf_blob (illustrative subset; mirror X for the right side):
  {
    "id": "figure", "type": "sdf_blob",
    "balls": [
      { "position": [ 0,    0.45, 0], "strength": 0.15, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [ 0,    0.15, 0], "strength": 0.25, "subtract": 10 },
      { "position": [-0.18, 0.20, 0], "strength": 0.10, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.20, 0.05, 0], "strength": 0.10, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.22,-0.10, 0], "strength": 0.09, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.24,-0.25, 0], "strength": 0.08, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.08, 0.00, 0], "strength": 0.10, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.10,-0.20, 0], "strength": 0.10, "subtract": 12 },
      { "position": [-0.12,-0.40, 0], "strength": 0.10, "subtract": 12 }
    ],
    "resolution": 48, "scale": [1.0, 1.0, 0.55],
    "smoothing": "smooth", "material": "matte", "baseColor": "#f0f1f3",
    "roughness": 0.75, "metalness": 0.0
  }

If limbs still read as too short after this: add more balls per limb, or
push the lowest ball(s) further along the limb axis — don't just scale
the whole object, which widens everything uniformly instead of lengthening
the limb.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
61.4 — COMMON MISTAKES QUICK-REFERENCE (organic/sculpted references)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
  ✗ "I'll just position primitives close together." → 61.1: use sdf_blob
    (organic) or CSG boolean union (hard-surface), not proximity.
  ✗ "One ball per arm is enough." → 61.3: use 3-5 balls per limb in a
    vertical line.
  ✗ "detail 2 on the sphere should be smooth enough." → use detail 3
    (primitives) or resolution 48 (sdf_blob) for anything sculpted; lower
    values show visible facets (61.5).
  ✗ "I'll use the 'flat' material for a clean look." → "flat" forces flat
    shading; use "matte" or set "smoothing":"smooth" for sculpted/organic
    surfaces (PART 26.3).
  ✗ "The arms are too short, I'll scale them up." → if the arm is really
    a separate capsule that doesn't merge with the body, scaling it
    doesn't fix the underlying seam — see 61.1.
  ✗ "The head is floating with a gap, I'll move it closer." → two
    separate primitives always keep two separate surfaces, however close
    — merge with sdf_blob instead of nudging position.
  ✗ "I'll add a neck cylinder to connect head and body." → a neck
    cylinder is a third separate primitive; it hides the seam, it
    doesn't remove it.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
61.5 — CROSS-REFERENCE: DETAIL/RESOLUTION AND MATERIAL FOR SCULPTED LOOKS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Restating the two knobs that most often get left at a default too low for
a sculpted reference (both already documented at PART 2/12 and PART 3/5/
26.3 — repeated here because 61.4's mistakes list them so often):
  - detail: 0-3 on ordinary primitives (3 = max, e.g. 1280 faces on a
    sphere). resolution: 16-48 on sdf_blob (48 = max marching-cubes
    smoothness). Use the max on sculpted/hero parts — low values leave
    visible facets that break the sculpted read.
  - For a soft matte sculpted surface: "material":"matte", moderate-to-
    high "roughness" (0.5-0.75), "metalness" near 0, and "smoothing":
    "smooth" for guaranteed Gouraud shading rather than relying on
    "auto". Reserve "toon"/"flat" for a deliberately flat-shaded/toon
    look, never as a default for a sculpted reference.

================================================================================
PART 62 — v8.0 ADDITIONS: MODELING & TEXTURING TECHNIQUE VOCABULARY AUDIT
          (source: 3d_modeling_texturing_techniques.txt — a 35-technique
          reference list of code/procedural CG modeling and texturing
          methods, checked term-by-term against the actual renderer source
          on 2026-07-30)
================================================================================
This is a different list from PART 54's 20 named techniques and from the
13-technique point-cloud/mesh-reconstruction pass referenced in PART 46 —
some terms overlap (CSG, SDF, triplanar, UV mapping, AO), most don't. Where
a technique was already covered by an earlier PART, this section says so
and points there instead of re-explaining it. Same tagging convention as
PART 54:
  [ALREADY LIVE]     the renderer already does this
  [ALREADY LIVE, partial]  the renderer does a real version of the idea,
                     but not the literal named technique — explained below
  [ASPIRATIONAL]     not implementable/not implemented as the real
                     technique in this single-file WebGL/Three.js
                     renderer — explained why, and pointed at the closest
                     real equivalent this renderer actually has, if any
Every field named below already existed before this PART — this is a
documentation-only audit, it adds no new renderer capability and no
existing blueprint's render changes because this PART exists.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
62.0 — STATUS TABLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
